While background leakages and pipe bursts in well maintained systems\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ngenerally amount to only 3-7% of the total water supplied, they can account for more than 50%\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nfor poorly maintained WDNs worldwide (Puust et al., 2010). Methods that support prompt\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ndetection and accurate localization of leakages are crucial to help water utilities implement\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ntimely mitigation measures and avoid unnecessary loss of water. Beyond the direct effect on\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nreducing water losses, effective leakage management strategies can avoid revenue losses and\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nother undesired effects, including contaminant infiltration, property damage, and WDN\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ninefficiencies.\r\nSeveral works in the literature proposed approaches for leakage detection and localization.\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nHowever, the ongoing digitalization of urban water systems (Makropoulos and Savic, 2019;\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nStewart et al., 2018), along with the development of distributed sensor networks and improved\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nreal-time communication, are fostering the development of a new generation of on-line,\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ndata-driven leakage identification methods that process data stored in the supervisory control and\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ndata acquisition (SCADA) system in real-time. A lack of studies that comparatively analyze and\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nbenchmark different leak identification and localization methods has culminated in the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\norganization of the Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods (BattLeDIM 2020;\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nVrachimis et al., 2019). The BattLeDIM is an international competition organized for the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\npurpose of comparing the performance of different leakage detection and localization methods\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nbased on time-to-detection and location accuracy.\r\nThis research develops a high-resolution pressure-driven method for leakage identification and\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nlocalization in WDNs and tests the approach using the benchmark dataset provided as part of the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nBattLeDIM. Our method is composed of two modules that operate sequentially. The first module\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nperforms ​leakage event identification​. Our leakage identification algorithm processes the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\npressure from SCADA data observed at different sensor nodes in a WDN and identifies time\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nhistory of leakage events by analyzing pressure differences between pairs of nodes. The model is\r\nPAGE 2\r\ntrained using pressure data observed for a “normal” time period, i.e., a period without leak events\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\noccurring in the WDN. Leakages are then detected through the assessment of node pressure\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ndifferences on a time series that potentially includes one or more simultaneous leak events. The\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nreconstruction error from the model is analyzed to detect the anomalies, i.e., the leakages, in the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nobserved WDN. The model can be recalibrated to include each newly detected leak into the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n“normal” situation, enabling it to detect overlapping leakage states. At the same time,\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nconventional mass balance considerations are included where demand information is present to\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nsupport leakage identification and characterization. This enables experts to identify leak\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\noccurrences on a 5 min resolution and monitor its development over time.\r\nTo demonstrate the functionality of the leakage identification algorithm, two examples are\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nchosen to illustrate different scenarios (see Figures 1 and 2). For better visualization, the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\npresented signals are denoised by moving average, considering an averaging period of 1 day. The\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nfirst example, further referred to as ​Burst​, represents a scenario where a pipe burst is detected.\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nThe state change observed in Figure 1 can be identified as a clear rupture in the reconstruction\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nerror signal which occurs at the time step indicated by the red dashed line. Considering the signal\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ndelay of 1 day due to the moving averaging, an immediate new and stable state is reached at a\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nconsistent level of the reconstruction error. Moreover, it can be observed that the leak is fixed at\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\na later time (green dashed line), as the system returns to its previous state.\r\n\r\nFigure 1: Model reconstruction error for the identification of a sample ​Burst (blue line). The shaded red area\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nrepresents the time of occurrence of the leak. The estimated start and end of the leak are represented with dashed red\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nand green lines, respectively.\r\nContrary to the rupture caused in the ​Burst ​scenario, holes in pipe walls might grow in size\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nslowly and entail a rather smooth transition to a leakage state. This may be observed for the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nfollowing scenario, further referred to as ​Transient leak ​(Figure 2).\r\nPAGE 3\r\nFigure 2: Model reconstruction error for the identification of a sample ​Transient leak (blue line). The shaded red\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\narea represents the time of occurrence of the leak. The estimated start and end of the leak are represented with\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ndashed red and green lines, respectively.\r\nThe model reconstruction error in Figure 2 shows this transition as a transient growth process.\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nConsequently, the start of the growth process is not as evident as for a ​Burst​. In this case, an\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nexpert-based analysis of the reconstruction error is needed in our method to estimate the start\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ntime of the leak. The growth process reaches a final state at which growth stops. This final state\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nindicates that the considered hole in the pipe wall has stopped growing, possibly due to reaching\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nthe size of the pipe diameter. Finally, a fix is also visible for this example (dashed green line),\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nafter which the system returns to its normal state.\r\nThe leakage annotations from the leakage identification module are then used by the second\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nmodule\r\nof\r\nour\r\nproposed\r\nmethod,\r\nwhich\r\nperforms\r\n​leakage\r\nlocalization​.\r\nThe\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nsimulation-optimization framework developed in Berglund et al. (2017) is adapted to locate\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nleakages by the application of an iterative mixed-integer linear programming (MILP), which\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nminimizes the absolute differences between observed (SCADA) and hydraulic model simulated\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\npressure values reported at the sensors. To incorporate seasonal demand variation in the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nhydraulic model, the base demands and demand patterns (for one full year) are updated in the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ninput file based on AMR readings present in DMA C. Figure 3 shows the annual consumption\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nreported by AMR data and the modeled demand before and after editing the base demands and\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nthe consumption pattern multipliers. The framework relies on the pressure response of a set of\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ncandidate pipes when a fixed leak value is inserted at the center of each candidate pipe. The\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ncandidate pipes are selected based on the pressure drop reported by the sensors, and candidate\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\npipes are included in a search area located around the most affected sensor. In case the selection\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nof candidate pipes does not include the real pipe, the output of the model reports the pipe closest\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nto the true leaking pipe. Figure 4 shows, as an example, the candidate pipes location for the ​Burst\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nand ​Transient leak ​events identified on the first stage of the procedure. The set of candidate pipes\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ndoes not vary across each simulation-optimization run. A series of linear constraints are applied\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nto ensure that a solution is identified that yields the estimated leak magnitudes that minimize the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\npressure error reported from the set of candidate pipes.\r\nPAGE 4\r\nTwo challenges were identified for the localization step. The first one is related to the candidate\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\npipes selection, which is a key input of the optimization model. To address this issue, we relied\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\non the assumption that the most affected pressure sensor should be located closest to the leak.\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nBased on that, we explore several options for the pipes surrounding the most affected pressure\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nsensor. Second, as pointed out by Berglund et al. (2017), the iterative MILP method is suitable\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nfor error-free data, and for this application, we needed to match the output of the hydraulic model\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nwith the SCADA data. However, as data at the node-level is available only for one district of the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nnetwork, we are assuming that the rest of the nodes have a similar consumption pattern to that\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nobserved in DMA C.\r\n\r\nFigure 3: Daily water demand reported by AMR data aggregated at the district level over a year. (Blue) Original\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ndemand from SCADA data. (Orange) Updated demand of the WNTR hydraulic model, with edited base demand and\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\npattern multipliers for a time horizon of one year. (Green) Original demand of the WNTR hydraulic model.\r\nPAGE 5\r\nFigure 4: Candidate pipes (cyan) shown for the identified (a) ​Burst and (b) ​Transient leak events based on the most\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\naffected pressure sensor.\r\nOur pressure-driven method for leakage identification and localization is tested on the SCADA\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ndata provided for the benchmark WDN of L-Town (BattLeDIM, 2020). L-Town is a\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nmedium-sized network featuring two reservoirs that supply water to a network with pipes\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ndeveloping for a length of 42.6 km, 782 junctions, 1 water tank, 1 pump, and 3 pressure\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nreduction valves. Thirty-three pressure sensors and 82 Automated Meter Reading (AMR) sensors\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nprovide demand and pressure information to the SCADA system with a sampling frequency of 5\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nminutes. SCADA data for the L-Town WDN are provided for 2018 and 2019, along with\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nground-truth labeled leakage locations and fixes for 2018. Preliminary experiments show that our\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\npressure-driven method can promptly detect and localize most of the labeled leakages for 2018.\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nThe absence of minimum requirements regarding the temporal frequency of pressure and\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ndemand data sampling makes it suitable for real-time applications.\r\nPAGE 6\r\nSavic, D., Makropoulos, C., Kossieris, P., and Liu, A. (2018). Integrated intelligent water-energy\r\nmetering systems and informatics: Visioning a digital multi-utility service provider.\r\nEnvironmental Modelling & Software, 105, 94-117.\r\nVrachimis, S., Eliades, D., Taormina, T., Ostfeld, A., Kapelan, Z., Liu, S., Kyriakou, M.,\r\nPavlou, P., Qiu, M., and Polycarpou, M. (2019). Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation\r\nMethods (BattLeDIM 2020). URL: ​http://battledim.ucy.ac.cy\r\n\r\nKeywords:​ water loss, leak detection and localization, changepoint detection,\r\nsimulation-optimization, BattLeDIM\r\nPAGE 7\r\nSUMMARY\r\nWater losses are one of the main consequences of infrastructure failures in water distribution\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nnetworks (WDNs), accounting for more than 50% in some WDNs worldwide. Methods that\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nsupport prompt detection and accurate localization of leakages are crucial to help water utilities\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nimplement timely mitigation measures and avoid unnecessary losses of water and revenues. This\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nresearch develops a high-resolution pressure-driven method for leakage identification and\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nlocalization in WDNs and tests it using the benchmark dataset provided as part of the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\"BattLeDIM\", an international competition on leakage detection and localization. Our method is\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ncomposed of two modules that operate sequentially. The first module performs leakage\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nidentification by processing pressure data observed at different sensor nodes in a WDN and by\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nanalyzing pressure differences between pairs of nodes. The model is trained using pressure data\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nobserved for a “normal” time period, i.e., without leak events occurring in the WDN. The\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nreconstruction error from the model is then analyzed to detect the anomalies on a time series that\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\npotentially includes one or more simultaneous leak events. The leakage annotations from the\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nleakage identification module are then used by the second module, which performs leakage\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nlocalization. A simulation-optimization framework is adapted to locate leakages by iterative\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nmixed-integer linear programming. The framework relies on the pressure response of a set of\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\ncandidate pipes when a fixed leak value is inserted there. The candidate pipes are selected based\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\non the pressure drop reported by the sensors, and candidate pipes are included in a search area\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nlocated around the most affected sensor. Our proposed method is tested on the SCADA data\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nprovided with a 5-minute sampling frequency for the benchmark medium-sized WDN of\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nL-Town. Preliminary experiments show that our pressure-driven method can promptly detect and\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nlocalize most of the labeled leakages and it is suitable for real-time applications.\r\n\r\nDOCUMENT: Z3923501_IRI.txt\r\nPAGE 1\r\nLEAK LOCALIZATION IN WATER DISTRIBUTION\r\nNETWORKS USING DATA-DRIVEN AND MODEL-BASED\r\nAPPROACHES\r\n\r\nLuis Romero1, Joaquim Blesa1,2, Débora Alves2, Gabriela Cembrano1, Vicenç Puig1,2, Eric\r\nDuviella3\r\n1 Institut de Robòtica i Informàtica Industrial (CSIC-UPC). Carrer Llorens Artigas, 4-6, 08028\r\nBarcelona, Spain\r\n2 Supervision, Safety and Automatic Control Research Center (CS2AC) of the Universitat\r\nPolitècnica de Catalunya, Campus de Terrassa, Gaia Building, Rambla Sant Nebridi, 22, 08222\r\nTerrassa, Barcelona, Spain\r\n3 Informatics and Automatics Department, IMT Lille Douai, Lille, France\r\n\r\n1luis.romero.ben@upc.edu\r\n\r\nABSTRACT\r\n\r\nThe worldwide growing demand of water supply requires a proper management of the available\r\nhydraulic resources. One of the major concerns in the operation of water distribution networks\r\n(WDNs) is the existence of leakages, due to the high operational costs for the water utilities. Leaks\r\ncan produce substantial economic losses, infrastructure damage and even health risks. Therefore, leak\r\ndetection and isolation methodologies are widely researched.\r\n\r\nOne the one hand, model-based approaches exploit the existence of a hydraulic model of the\r\nconsidered WDN, as well as the availability of hydraulic measurements like inlet flow and pressure,\r\nand sensorized inner nodes pressure, to tackle the leak localization task. The suitability of these\r\nmethods has been confirmed by numerous works during the years. On the other hand, the sources of\r\ninformation in the majority of water networks are rather limited, and other interesting measurements\r\nare not available, like water demands at the junctions, flows between inner nodes, etc. Thus, data-\r\ndriven approaches, which have a reduced or non-existent dependency on a hydraulic model, can be\r\nhelpful to locate leaks in WDNs that lack the mentioned measurements and modelling.\r\n\r\nThis abstract presents the combined utilizat…2409 tokens truncated…odology was used for the\r\nBattLeDIM 2020 challenge, and was applied, as stated, for the training and testing datasets. About\r\nthe training results, some key aspects can be highlighted:\r\n\r\n-\r\nAbout the data-driven method, the eight leaks of 2018 in Area A, that are said to be located\r\nand fixed by the water utility of L-TOWN, were found by the leak localization approach, as\r\nthey were contained by the candidates set. There was a mean distance of 5.6 pipes from the\r\nleak to the best candidate, regarding that two of the leaks (p232 & p369) highly increase this\r\nmean value. If they are not considered, the mean is reduced to 3.5 pipes. It was discovered\r\nthat this behaviour is caused by the pipe distance from the leaks to the sensors. If considering\r\nonly the problematic leaks, the mean distance from the nearest sensors to the leak is 337.1 m,\r\nwhile this value is 158.7 m for the mean of the rest of the leaks.\r\n-\r\nAbout the model-based technique, the reported leak of 2018 in Area C was detected and\r\nlocated at the pipe level. In addition, a non-reported leak was also detected and located. On\r\nthe other hand, the leak of 2018 in Area B, was detected and located with an error of one pipe.\r\n\r\nThe testing results are included at the attached text file. The 2019 dataset implies a higher level of\r\ndifficulty, due to the existence of several incipient leaks that are not fixed, which hinder the\r\nperformance of the data-driven method, as well as the leak estimation for the model-based method.\r\n\r\nThe proposed approach is applicable to a broad class of water networks and leak situations, since it\r\nconsiders different options, e.g. for networks where no hydraulic model is available, taking advantage\r\nof a deployment of pressure sensors, or for networks with AMR and a reliable hydraulic model.\r\nSimilarly, it considers single-leak and multiple-leak situations.\r\n\r\nDOCUMENT: Z3964167_Cheng00.txt\r\nPAGE 1\r\n1\r\nA Hybrid Leakage Detection and Isolation Approach Based on\r\nEnsemble Multivariate Changepoint Detection Methods\r\n\r\nTuoyuan Cheng1, Yuanzhe Li2, Fouzi Harrou3, Ying Sun3, Jinliang Gao2,*, TorOve Leiknes1\r\n1 Water Desalination and Reuse Center, Biological and Environmental Science and Engineering\r\nDivision, King Abdullah University of Science and Technology, Thuwal, 23955-6900 Saudi Arabia\r\n2 School of Environment, Harbin Institute of Technology, Harbin 150090, China\r\n3 Computer, Electrical and Mathematical Sciences and Engineering Division, King Abdullah\r\nUniversity of Science and Technology, Thuwal, Saudi Arabia\r\n* gjl@hit.edu.cn\r\n\r\nABSTRACT\r\n\r\nEarly leakage detection and isolation are of paramount importance to the maintenance and resilience\r\nof the water distribution system (WDS). Efficient and accurate leakage detection and isolation\r\nalgorithms could support practitioners to optimize WDS design and operation. Modern WDS, as\r\nequipped with supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA), could record nodal measurements\r\nto facilitate training, validation, and selection of both mechanism-based and data-driven models.\r\n\r\nIn this study for the battle of the leakage detection and isolation methods, a hybrid approach based\r\non benchmark simulation and the ensemble multivariate changepoint detection (EMCPD) is proposed\r\nto detect leakage occurrences. Bilinear bivariate spatial interpolation for irregular spaced data and\r\ntwo-sample one-sided Student’s t-test is further invocated to isolate leakage sites.\r\n\r\nFirst, a simulation was performed, assuming no leakage occurred to attain the benchmark working\r\ncondition series of the L-town WDS. The pressure-dependent demand scheme was deployed via the\r\ntoolkit, water network tool for resilience (WNTR), with a simulation time step of five minutes [1].\r\nUpon simulation, the residuals of flowrates and pressures were calculated by contrasting the sensor\r\nmeasurements and the simulated results. The observed demand data were assimilated as the nodal\r\npattern, and therefore the nodal demand residuals were omitted.\r\n\r\nBased on the flowrate residuals, the EMCPD was performed to detect the rough time of leak events.\r\nSince the three flowrate sensors were installed either by the pump or by the reservoirs, the flowrate\r\nresiduals should offer direct evidence of leakage events. At this stage, the flowrate residuals series\r\nwith three variables were averaged to three observations per day in order to denoise short term\r\nfluctuation, keep diurnal and weekly cycles, and accelerate the computation.\r\n\r\nAround each rough candidate, the EMCPDs were performed to distinguish accurate moment\r\ncandidates. At this stage, both the flowrate residuals (three variables) and pressure residuals (34\r\nvariables, tank water level included) series were engaged at five minutes level, from 46 hours before\r\nthe rough timing to 2 hours after the rough timing. With more covariates involved, the multivariate\r\nresiduals time series would shrink the accurate candidates’ pool.\r\n\r\nFinally, around each accurate moment candidate, the pressure contours of the L-town were calculated\r\nby adopting bilinear bivariate spatial interpolation based on the monitored 34 pressure time series [2].\r\nTo be conservative, only the nodal pressures inside the convex hull of pressure sensors were estimated\r\nfrom 25 minutes before each accurate candidate moment to 25 minutes after each accurate candidate\r\nmoment. For each node, its estimated pressures time series were firstly exponentially moving\r\naveraged and then grouped as before or after the candidate moment. The two-sample one-sided\r\nStudent’s t-tests with unknown variance were performed on the two groups to isolate the most likely\r\nsites. The sites would be characterized as showing the minimal p-values, or with the most significant\r\nnegative mean pressure shift between two groups. Since all pressures were estimated based on 34\r\nPAGE 2\r\n2\r\nsparse sensors, bilinear spatial interpolation without extrapolation, as well as a p-value threshold of\r\n0.01 were adopted, with the intention to avoid overfitting, to be conservative at un-monitored nodes,\r\nand to be parsimonious at raising alarms.\r\n\r\nThe EMCPD was performed by involving the results of six algorithms, including the most recent\r\nchangepoint detection method [3], the non-parametric multiple changepoint analysis methods (with\r\nthe E statistic or the Kolmogorov-Smirnov statistic) [4], the divisive hierarchical estimation algorithm,\r\nthe kernel changepoint analysis, and the Bayesian estimator of abrupt change and trends [5]. The most\r\nrecent changepoint detection method analyzed univariate time-series independently to obtain a\r\nprofile-likelihood measures that summarizes the evidence of changepoints and then pooled\r\ninformation across time-series to give an optimal set. The non-parametric MCPD involved dynamic\r\nprogramming and pruning without underlying distributional assumptions. The Bayesian method\r\nquantified the relative usefulness of encompassed individual decomposition models, and then\r\nleveraged them via Bayesian model averaging. The votes from individual MCPDs were gathered to\r\nelect the most likely candidate per detection turn. Hyperparameters were tuned according to the 2018\r\ndataset.\r\n\r\nThe proposed approach perceived 30 rough candidates and 55 accurate candidate moments, as\r\ntabulated in Tab.1. The vote counts from EMCPD was relatively scattered for the rough candidates,\r\nbut highly concentrated for the accurate candidates. The flowrate residuals as well as rough candidates\r\nwere delineated in Fig.1. Four instances of pressure changes around an accurate candidate were\r\ngraphed in Fig.2. The Fig.2(a) presented an abrupt pressure drop prior and posterior to an accurate\r\nmoment candidate, which could be due to a pipe burst event. The Fig.2(b) presented a minor pressure\r\ndrop prior and posterior to an accurate moment candidate, which could be due to the downward\r\npressure trend drifting lead by the development of a background leakage. The Fig.2(c) presented an\r\nabrupt pressure rise prior and posterior to an accurate moment candidate, which could be due to the\r\nrepair of an existing pipe burst event. The Fig.2(d) presented a minor pressure rise prior and posterior\r\nto an accurate moment candidate, which could be due to the upward pressure trend drifting. The\r\nisolation outcomes together with one-sided Student’s t-test results based on exponentially moving\r\naveraged short-term pressure time series around accurate moment candidates were listed in Tab. 2.\r\n\r\nTable 1. 14:15:00\r\np352\r\n884.82\r\n1295.43\r\n0.0013\r\n29.74\r\n29.53\r\n-0.20\r\n\r\nThe proposed hybrid approach employed both mechanistic model-based WNTR simulation and data-\r\ndriven two-step EMCPD methods for leakage detection. Demand records data were assimilated to\r\nmatch the mechanistic WNTR model with the observations, and output residuals for detection. To\r\ndecrease computation costs, the EMCPD was applied in two steps with different temporal resolutions,\r\nwhich avoided iteratively solving hydraulic simulations. During the isolation step, to be conservative\r\nand parsimonious at triggering alarms among the sparse sensor network, lower-order interpolation\r\nfrom observed pressure data with no extrapolation, as well as a low p-value threshold were adopted.\r\nThe overall approach with limited assumptions on the distribution of the underlying dataset was both\r\ncomputationally efficient and conveniently transferable to other spatial-temporal datasets and\r\nmechanistic models. Future research could adopt spatial kriging methods to explore leakage isolation\r\nvia interpolation among sparse sensors.\r\n\r\nKeywords: Leakage detection and isolation; hybrid modeling; ensemble multivariate changepoint\r\ndetection; bilinear spatial interpolation; hypothesis testing\r\nPAGE 6\r\n6\r\nSUMMARY\r\n\r\nA hybrid approach based on benchmark simulation and the ensemble multivariate changepoint\r\ndetection (EMCPD) is proposed to detect leakage occurrences. Bilinear bivariate spatial interpolation\r\nand Student’s t-test is further invocated to isolate leakage sites.\r\n\r\nFirst, a simulation was performed, assuming no leakage occurred to attain the benchmark working\r\ncondition series of the L-town WDS. The pressure-dependent demand scheme was deployed with a\r\nsimulation time step of five minutes. Upon simulation, the residuals of flowrates and pressures were\r\ncalculated by contrasting the sensor measurements and the simulated results. The observed demand\r\ndata were assimilated as the nodal pattern, and therefore the nodal demand residuals were omitted.\r\n\r\nBased on the flowrate residuals, the EMCPD was performed to detect the rough time of leak events.\r\nAt this stage, the flowrate residuals series were averaged to three observations per day to denoise\r\nshort term fluctuation, keep diurnal and weekly cycles, and accelerate the computation.\r\n\r\nAround each rough candidate, the EMCPDs were performed to distinguish accurate candidates. At\r\nthis stage, both the flowrate residuals and pressure residuals series were engaged at five minutes level.\r\n\r\nFinally, around each accurate candidate, the nodal pressures were calculated by using bilinear spatial\r\ninterpolation based on monitored pressure time series. The two-sample one-sided Student’s t-tests\r\nwere performed to isolate the most likely sites. Since all pressures were estimated based on 34 sparse\r\nsensors, bilinear bivariate interpolation without extrapolation, as well as a p-value threshold of 0.01\r\nwere adopted, with the intention to avoid overfitting, to be conservative at un-monitored nodes, and\r\nto be parsimonious at raising alarms.\r\n\r\nThe overall approach with limited assumptions on the underlying dataset distribution was both\r\ncomputationally efficient and conveniently transferable to other spatial-temporal datasets and\r\nmechanistic models. Future research could adopt spatial kriging to explore leakage isolation via water\r\npressure interpolation among sparse sensors.\r\n"

Therefore, leak\r\ndetection and isolation methodologies are widely researched.\r\n\r\nOne the one hand, model-based approaches exploit the existence of a hydraulic model of the\r\nconsidered WDN, as well as the availability of hydraulic measurements like inlet flow and pressure,\r\nand sensorized inner nodes pressure, to tackle the leak localization task. The suitability of these\r\nmethods has been confirmed by numerous works during the years. On the other hand, the sources of\r\ninformation in the majority of water networks are rather limited, and other interesting measurements\r\nare not available, like water demands at the junctions, flows between inner nodes, etc. Thus, data-\r\ndriven approaches, which have a reduced or non-existent dependency on a hydraulic model, can be\r\nhelpful to locate leaks in WDNs that lack the mentioned measurements and modelling.\r\n\r\nThis abstract presents the combined utilization of a model-based and a novel data-driven methodology\r\nto locate leaks in the concrete case of the challenge proposed at BattLeDIM 2020. The division of the\r\nintroduced network (L-Town) in three areas allows to determine the usage of one of the approaches\r\nat each one of these areas, depending on their concrete characteristics.\r\n\r\nBesides, both methods allow to solve the multi-leak problem in a proper way, which entails a further\r\nstep with regard to the classical single-leak assumption.\r\n\r\nKeywords: leak localization, water distribution network, model-based, data-driven, multi-leak\r\nproblem\r\nINTRODUCTION\r\n\r\nIn the last ten years, our research team, with a solid experience in fault diagnosis applied to different\r\nreal applications, have developed several leak localization methods that include model-based methods\r\n(Pérez et al., 2011), data-driven methods (Soldevila et al., 2020) and hybrid methods (Soldevila et al.,\r\n2016), which have successfully been applied to real WDNs. These methods considered inlet pressure\r\nand flow measurements, that are usually gathered in WDN for control purposes, and some pressure\r\nmeasurements in inner nodes from installed sensors that are easy and relatively cheap to install. The\r\nPAGE 2\r\nL-Town challenge in BattLeDIM 2020 introduces multi-leak scenarios including abrupt and incipient\r\nleaks. Additionally, the three areas composing the network present different kind and amount of\r\nsensors. These facts have motivated the development of two new leak localization methods: A new\r\ndata-driven approach that benefits from the high density of pressure sensors installed in Area A and\r\na new model-based method that benefits from demand measurements provided by Automated\r\nMetered Readings (AMRs) in most of the nodes of Area C. The model-based method has also been\r\napplied to Area B, where there is a very low density of pressure measurements to apply the data-\r\ndriven method. In the following, both leak localization methods that are able to tackle the multi-leak\r\nproblem are described in more detail.\r\n\r\nDATA-DRIVEN APPROACH\r\n\r\nAn explicative diagram of the novel data-driven leak localization procedure is displayed in Figure 1.\r\n\r\nFigure 1: Workflow scheme of the complete data-driven leak localization approach.\r\n\r\nIt consists of a two-phase process:\r\n\r\n1. From the hydraulic measurements gathered by the installed pressure sensors, as well as the\r\ntopology of the WDN, the complete state of the network is estimated. The sensorized nodes\r\nstate value will correspond to their exact hydraulic head, whereas the state of the rest of the\r\nnodes will consist of an approximation of the actual hydraulic head.\r\n2. From the analysis of the current and nominal complete states, achieved from the network state\r\ninterpolation of the current leaky data and historical non-leaky data respectively, a specific\r\narea of the network is considered as the source of the leak. An estimation of a more precise\r\nlocation of the leak is derived from the distance metric that is exploited to select the candidates.\r\n\r\nAs mentioned above, this methodology has been applied to Area A, due to the existence of 29 sensors\r\ndistributed among the 659 nodes contained in the area. To face the presented challenge, the method\r\nis extended to locate pipe leaks by assuming the leak to be located in the pipe that connects the best\r\nnode candidates.\r\n\r\n1.1. Network state interpolation\r\n\r\nThe proposed state estimation method is based on two main ideas:\r\n\r\n1. To introduce a simplification in the relation between the states of nodes of the network. The\r\ninformation of the measured nodes would be extended to the rest of nodes by means of a simple\r\nand linear equation. Hence, the state of the network does not represent exact hydraulic\r\nvariables (except for the sensorized nodes), although it properly estimates them.\r\n2. To exploit the graph associated to the WDN structure to perform the estimation of the states.\r\nThis translates to the utilization of the nodes adjacency and the network flows directionality to\r\ninduce a better approximation of the states.\r\nPAGE 3\r\nBoth objectives can be reached by solving the following optimization problem to obtain the network\r\nstate estimator 𝒙:\r\n𝑚𝑖𝑛\r\n𝒙 1\r\n2 [𝒙𝑇𝐻𝒙+ 𝜏𝛾2]\r\n𝑠. 𝑡. 𝐵⋅𝒙≤𝜸\r\n𝑆⋅𝒙= 𝒙𝑠\r\n\r\nwhere 𝐻= 𝐿𝐷−2𝐿, and 𝐷 and 𝐿 are the degree matrix and the Laplacian of the graph associated\r\nto the WDN topology, respectively. In this way, the first part of the cost function introduces the\r\nsimplification of the relation among nodes by means of the harmonization property, i.e., 𝒙𝑇𝐻𝒙=\r\n∑\r\n[𝑥𝑖−\r\n1\r\n𝑑𝑖𝑖𝒘𝑖𝒙 ]\r\n𝑛\r\n𝑖=1\r\n2\r\n, being 𝑛 the total number of nodes. 𝒘𝑖 stands for the ith row of a weight\r\nmatrix 𝑊, achieved from the lengths of the pipes of the network (these distances must be inverted\r\nso that close nodes share a high weight value). The second part of the cost function, together with the\r\ninequality constraint, allow to impose a condition about the flow directionality by means of the\r\nincidence matrix 𝐵, in order to obtain more accurate state interpolations. Finally, the equality\r\nconstraint sets the sensorized nodes states to their respective head value, achieved from the sensors\r\nmeasurements.\r\n\r\nAn example of the obtained results by means of this interpolation method is shown in Figure 2.\r\n\r\nFigure 2: Comparison between the complete network state estimation, obtained for three different scenarios: a hydraulic\r\nsimulation of a nominal situation, a graph-based interpolation of a nominal situation and a graph-based interpolation of a\r\nsituation with a leak.\r\n\r\n1.2. Leak candidate selection method (LCSM)\r\n\r\nThe key idea of the second part of the method consists of considering the state of a healthy situation\r\nof the network, 𝒙𝑛𝑜𝑚, and the state of the scenario with leak, 𝒙𝑙𝑒𝑎𝑘, to be the 𝑥−𝑦 coordinates of\r\n𝑛 points in ℝ2. Then, the best fitting line for the obtained points cloud is computed, and the\r\nperpendicular distance, with sign, from each individual point to the line is computed. The importance\r\nof the sign lies in the assumption that leaky points tend to undergo a larger pressure value reduction\r\nin comparison to non-leaky nodes. This effect would be more noticeable in nodes which are close to\r\nthe leaky one. The produced line directly depends on the values of all the nodes, and hence the\r\ndistance between a certain node location in ℝ2 and this line not only encodes information about the\r\nchange of state in that node, but also the relation among states of nodes of the network.\r\n\r\nDue to the large set of nodes that may fulfil the requirement of having a positive distance value, they\r\nneed to be filtered. To solve this problem, the standard deviation of the distance vector is computed,\r\nusing the achieved value as a threshold.\r\nPAGE 4\r\nThe set of final candidates for the presented example is displayed in Figure 4. Additionally, a detailed\r\nimage is presented in the form of a colour map. The colour of each node depends on the distance\r\nvalue provided by the LCSM method: the greener the node, the higher the distance.\r\n\r\nFigure 3: Representation of the set of candidates (right) together with a colour map of the distance value for each candidate\r\n(left). The red ellipse encircles the actual leaky pipe, while the blue ellipse indicates the zone with higher distances in the\r\nLCSM method.\r\n\r\nThe proximity of the red and blue ellipses illustrates the good performance of the proposed method.\r\nNevertheless, notice that the goal of the method is to find a location area that contains the leak, but\r\nsome degree of error may exist.\r\n\r\nMODEL-BASED APPROACH\r\n\r\nThe model-based leak localization method developed and applied to Areas C and B relies on the\r\nscheme depicted in Fig. 4, based on computing pressures for different the leak scenarios (hypothesis)\r\nand matching actual pressure measurements with the most probable leak hypothesis.\r\n\r\nFigure 4: Workflow scheme of the model-based leak localization approach (left) and localization result for the fixed\r\nleak in Area C during 2018 (right).\r\n\r\nNote that pressures of leak hypothesis are computed by an Epanet WDN model that considers user\r\ndemands measured (if there are AMRs) and non-leak demands computed by the expected total\r\ndemand distribution of the effective inlet flow (measured inlet flow minus leak estimation\r\nmagnitudes) where there are not AMR measurements. In addition, the estimation of leaks produced\r\nin previous instants\r\n1\r\n1\r\n1\r\n1\r\n....\r\nN\r\nj\r\njN\r\nl\r\nl\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nare introduced in the nodes where the leak have been located in\r\nPAGE 5\r\nprevious steps and the magnitude of the leak produced at the current step\r\nN\r\nil\r\nis introduced in the\r\ndifferent possible leak places (hypothesis)\r\n1,...,\r\nn\r\ni\r\nn\r\n\r\n. Therefore, a leak detection algorithm is\r\nnecessary to determine at which time a leak is present in every area and to provide an estimation of\r\nits magnitude. In this work, leak detection and estimation is carried out by means the analysis of two\r\ninlet flows evolutions: the total inlet flow minus the pump flow to reservoir of Area C to detect and\r\nestimate leaks in area A and B and the output flow of reservoir of Area C compared with AMRs\r\nmeasurements to detect and estimate leaks in Area C.\r\n\r\nBATTLE APPLICATION and CONCLUSIONS\r\n\r\nThis abstract presents a combined leak localization model-based/data-driven approach, applying each\r\ntechnique depending on the characteristics of the network. This methodology was used for the\r\nBattLeDIM 2020 challenge, and was applied, as stated, for the training and testing datasets. About\r\nthe training results, some key aspects can be highlighted:\r\n\r\n-\r\nAbout the data-driven method, the eight leaks of 2018 in Area A, that are said to be located\r\nand fixed by the water utility of L-TOWN, were found by the leak localization approach, as\r\nthey were contained by the candidates set. There was a mean distance of 5.6 pipes from the\r\nleak to the best candidate, regarding that two of the leaks (p232 & p369) highly increase this\r\nmean value. If they are not considered, the mean is reduced to 3.5 pipes. It was discovered\r\nthat this behaviour is caused by the pipe distance from the leaks to the sensors. If considering\r\nonly the problematic leaks, the mean distance from the nearest sensors to the leak is 337.1 m,\r\nwhile this value is 158.7 m for the mean of the rest of the leaks.\r\n-\r\nAbout the model-based technique, the reported leak of 2018 in Area C was detected and\r\nlocated at the pipe level. In addition, a non-reported leak was also detected and located. On\r\nthe other hand, the leak of 2018 in Area B, was detected and located with an error of one pipe.\r\n\r\nThe testing results are included at the attached text file. The 2019 dataset implies a higher level of\r\ndifficulty, due to the existence of several incipient leaks that are not fixed, which hinder the\r\nperformance of the data-driven method, as well as the leak estimation for the model-based method.\r\n\r\nThe proposed approach is applicable to a broad class of water networks and leak situations, since it\r\nconsiders different options, e.g. for networks where no hydraulic model is available, taking advantage\r\nof a deployment of pressure sensors, or for networks with AMR and a reliable hydraulic model.\r\nSimilarly, it considers single-leak and multiple-leak situations.\r\n\r\nDOCUMENT: P04a_CIACUA.txt\r\nPAGE 1\r\n2nd International CCWI / WDSA Joint Conference, Beijing, China – November 12-15, 2020\r\nBattle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods:\r\nAn Energy Method Analysis using Genetic Algorithms\r\n\r\nJuan Saldarriaga1, Laura Solarte2, Camilo Salcedo3, Carlos Montes4, Laura Martínez5, María\r\nGonzález6, María Cuello7, Andrés Ariza8, Camilo Galindo9, Néstor Ortiz10, Cristian Gómez11\r\n& Sergio Vanegas12\r\nUniversidad de los Andes, Civil and Environmental Engineering Department and Water Supply and\r\nSewerage Systems Research Center (CIACUA), Carrera 1 Este # 19A - 40, Bogotá, Colombia\r\n1jsaldarr@uniandes.edu.co, 2lm.solarte@uniandes.edu.co, 3ca.salcedo959@uniandes.edu.co,\r\n4cd.montes1256@uniandes.edu.co, 5ls.martinez10@uniandes.edu.co,\r\n6ma.gonzalezm1@uniandes.edu.co, 7mm.cuello547@uniandes.edu.co, 8ad.ariza@uniandes.edu.co,\r\n9c.galindo@uniandes.edu.co, 10nv.ortiz@uniandes.edu.co, 11cc.gomez@uniandes.edu.co &\r\n12sm.vanegas@uniandes.edu.co\r\nKeywords: Leakages, emitters, Genetic Algorithms, EPANET, water networks.\r\n\r\n1. Context: Problem approach\r\n\r\nLeakages in water networks are a common issue in urban water supply infrastructure. These leakages\r\ncan occur in pipe junctions and sections and in pipes or accessories that are part of\r\nstorage/compensation tanks. Usually, leakages can be caused by ground movement or settling due to\r\ntraffic or poor-quality materials. In addition, pressure fluctuations in the network, pressure transitions,\r\nor excessive pressures are a common cause of failures leading to leakages.\r\n\r\nWhen the behavior of leaks is analyzed, it can be seen that they are dependent of the demand,\r\nsomething that varies along the day, because during low demand periods, network pressures are\r\nhigher, and this results in larger leaks discharges. This behavior can be noted in Figure 1 and Figure\r\n2, the first shows fluctuations of flow along a 24h period for a node before a leak event, after it and\r\nthe case after leak detection using a hydraulic model. Figure 2 shows how a higher pressure generates\r\na higher demand and leakage. The equation from an emitter can be used to simulate leakage flowrate\r\nin a particular node in a water distribution network (WDN) (Wu, et al., 2010).\r\n\r\nFigure 1.Comparison between flowrate before and after\r\na leak detection throughout a day. Wu et al, 2010.\r\n\r\nFigure 2.Relation between pressure, demand and leakage in\r\nnode i. Gupta et al, 2016.\r\nThe methodology used to address the objective of this battle requires a network calibrated hydraulic\r\nmodeling, which requires detailed information of topological, topographical and hydraulic variables.\r\nWhen calibrating the model, possible leaks are simulated in the nodes using emitters, where the leak\r\nflowrate is assumed to be a function of the pressure at that point as indicated in the Equation 1.\r\n𝑄𝑖= 𝜀𝑖𝑖𝑃𝑖\r\n𝑋 [Equation 1]\r\nPAGE 2\r\n2nd International CCWI / WDSA Joint Conference, Beijing, China – November 12-15, 2020\r\nwhere 𝑄𝑖 is the aggregated leakage flow in the node i, 𝑃𝑖 is the pressure in the node i, and 𝜀𝑖𝑖 is\r\nthe emitter coefficient (it varies in each node) in the node and X the emitter exponent (constant for\r\nall nodes in the case study). Pressure variation in a particular node is different (less) when there is an\r\nemitter in it representing a leakage, compared with pressure variation without that leakage. This\r\ndifference plus the difference in discharge curves can be used to locate leakages. Emitter exponent\r\ncan vary from 0.5 to 2.5 depending on pipe material; it has been determined that the correct value to\r\ndetermine leakage and failure in metallic pipes is of 0.5 (Lambert, 2002), a value used as constant in\r\nour approach. On the other hand, an emitter coefficient larger than zero (0) will result in a leak on the\r\nnode. That means that 𝜀𝑖 of each node must be calculated as an indicator of possible leaks in order\r\nto explain changes in pressure and discharge curves. The basic idea is to use a heuristic to find 𝜀𝑖 to\r\neach node (most of them are 0) and those cases with values greater than zero indicate that there is a\r\npossibility that the pipes connected to the node could have a leak.\r\n\r\n2. Data: Patterns and filtration\r\n\r\nThe available information that describes the hydraulic behavior of L-Town network is classified into\r\nfour categories: pressure, flow at the DMA inflows and at the pump, demand in “Area C” and tanks\r\nwater level. In particular, the pressure data presents hydraulic information at internal points along the\r\nnetwork and presents patterns that are highly susceptible to change with the presence of leakages.\r\nTherefore, the pressure information was implemented for the analysis of the behavior of the leaks in\r\nthe network. This analysis had three components.\r\n\r\na. Pressure graphs generation and preliminary analysis.\r\nFrom the existing data, a graph for pressure vs. time could be obtained for different points. In these\r\ngraphs it is possible to identify different behavior patterns along a normal day, including maximum\r\nand minimum pressures, average pressure, rate of variation at different hours of the day, among other\r\ninformation.\r\n\r\nb. Anomaly detection.\r\nThe anomaly detection analysis is based on establishing differentiating points of behavior in existing\r\npressure data to identify the possible relation between anomalies and occurrence of leaks in the\r\nsystem. An example of the aforementioned can be seen on Figure 1, where there is a variation of\r\npressure close to 0.5 m in comparison with the behavior previously registered. The selection of\r\nparameters, if done in a precise way, will allow the identification of points suitable for study in the\r\nleakage occurrence analysis.\r\n\r\nc. Screening of available data\r\nThe same aforementioned process to obtain pressure graphs was done with discharge and tank level\r\ndata. An initial analysis took place to pave the way a balancing of the flowrates in the network;\r\nnevertheless, there was not a direct relation that could lead to the identifying of a specific anomaly in\r\nthis pattern. On another note, it can be seen that the existing data for water surface level only shows\r\none site which prevents an extrapolation throughout the entire system. Thus, a decision was made to\r\nabandon this approach using discharges and water levels, and our analysis was made using only\r\npressure data on some nodes and the a…9028 tokens truncated… 1,2, … , JD) (2)\r\n∆𝐻𝑘,𝑖 stands for water pressure change at node i when there is intermediate leakage ∆Q𝑘 in pipe segment\r\nk.\r\n∆𝐻𝑚𝑎𝑥 sands for maximum water pressure change at node i when there is intermediate leakage Q𝑘 in\r\npipe segment k (theoretically it should be one of the two ends of pipe segment k) .\r\nXk is used to represent characteristic value of pipe bust, The characteristic value of all pipe segments\r\nXk = (Xk,1 ,Xk,2 , … , Xk,JD ) is represented by the following matrix:\r\nXk ,i =\r\n[\r\n\r\nX1,1 X1,2 … X1,JD\r\nX2,1 X2,2 … X2,JD\r\n… … … …\r\nXp,1 Xp,2 … Xp,JD]\r\n\r\np×JD\r\n(3)\r\nThe matrix above is the characteristic value of all pipe burst under the ideal state. When the outlet flow\r\n∆Q𝑘 changes, the characteristic value of pipe burst will also change, but when the outlet flow of the burst pipe\r\nexceeds 4% of the total water supply, this change is not obvious. Therefore, when calculating the actual\r\ncharacteristic value of pipe burst of the pipe network, the average daily water leakage can be calculated\r\naccording to the size of the local water leakage rate. The average daily water leakage is taken as the outlet flow\r\nof the pipe burst in one pipe segment, and this group of characteristic values is taken as the characteristic values\r\nof all the burst pipes of the pipe network under the ideal state.\r\n2 Determination of Fuzzy Similarity Priority Ratio and Pipe Burst Location\r\n2.1 Introduction of Fuzzy Similarity Priority Ratio\r\nSimilarity priority ratio is a form of fuzziness measurement, which is to compare a pair of samples with\r\na fixed sample to determine which one is more similar to the fixed sample, so as to choose the one with a\r\ngreater degree of similarity to the fixed sample. In this paper, the paired samples are the characteristic values\r\nof pipe burst in P pipe segments of a fixed pipe network, and the fixed samples are the calculated characteristic\r\nvalues of pipe burst in a certain pipe segment when pipe burst occurs.\r\nAssuming that samples xi and x𝑗 are compared with fixed samples x𝑒, their similarity priority ratioR𝑖𝑗\r\nmust meet the requirements:In fuzzy priority ratio analysis, Hamming distance is generally adopted as a\r\nmeasurement of R𝑖𝑗 in similarity priority ratio. According to calculate the similarity priority ratio between\r\nthe two samples we obtain the fuzzy similarity matrix. After the fuzzy similarity matrix is established, the\r\nsimilarity sample is selected from the λ level set. In general, if there are m factors in each sample, there will\r\nbe a fuzzy similarity matrix for each factor. Therefore, each factor in each sample will generate an ordinal\r\nvalue reflecting the degree of similarity. Finally, the ordinal values of each factor in each sample will be added\r\nand the result is a comprehensive reflection of the similarity degree between the sample and the fixed sample.\r\n2.2 Positioning of Pipe Burst\r\n2.2.1 Similarity Analysis\r\nThe similar system refers to the existence of several similar elements between systems. The more similar\r\nelements there are between two systems, the greater the value of similar elements, and this will lead to a higher\r\nPAGE 4\r\ndegree of similarity between two systems.\r\nThe location of pipe burst can be seen as the similarity comparison between the corresponding\r\ncharacteristic values of pipe burst in pipe segment A of the pipe network and the characteristic values of pipe\r\nburst in each pipe segment (including pipe segment A) under the ideal state. It can be seen from figure 1 that\r\nwhen a pipe burst occurs in the same pipe network and in the same pipe, the amount of water leakage can\r\nchange the variation value of water pressure, but the overall change trend of water pressure at each node does\r\nnot change. If the pressure changes at each node caused by each change in water leakage are considered as a\r\nsystem, it shows that the systems are similar to each other.\r\n\r\nFigure 1. Node Pressure Changes in one pipe under Different Outlet Flow of Pipe Bursts\r\n2.2.2 Positioning\r\nFor a fixed pipe network, the daily average leakage quantity is taken as outlet flow of pipe burst of each\r\npipe segment to calculate the characteristic value matrix [x𝑘𝑖] of the burst pipe in the ideal state. The matrix\r\nis used as the sample to be identified. When the pressure at the pressure measuring point drops suddenly and\r\nexceeds the pressure warning line of the SCADA system, the SCADA system will send out an alarm message\r\nto estimate the status of the burst pipe and obtain the water pressure at each node. The characteristic value of\r\npipe burst is calculated according to equation (1). The characteristic value of the pipe burst is Xk\r\n′ =\r\n(Xk,1 ,Xk,2 , … , Xk,JD )and it is taken as a fixed sample. By comparing all the characteristic values of pipe burst\r\nin the ideal state with the characteristic values of pipe burst under the pipe burst state, that which characteristic\r\nvalues of pipe burst under the ideal state is more similar to the characteristic values of pipe burst under the\r\npipe burst state can be determined, consequently, those with higher degree of similarity with the characteristic\r\nvalues of pipe burst in the state of pipe burst are selected.\r\nPAGE 5\r\nFigure 2. Examples of the results of our location model for the 2018 pipe burst events\r\n\r\nKeywords: Water Distribution Network; Pipe Burst Identification; Location; Characteristic Values;\r\nSimilarity Analysis\r\n\r\nSUMMARY\r\n\r\nDrinking Water Distribution Networks (DWDN) are susceptible to infrastructure failures, which may lead\r\nto water losses. The early detection and localization of some leakage event is extremely important, as this\r\nwould reduce the time required for accommodating the event and therefore reducing the risk of further\r\ninfrastructure degradation, contamination events and consumer complaints. In recent years, large and medium-\r\nsized cities in China has successively set out the Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition(SCADA) system\r\nfor water supply pipe network, but most of the cities still cannot make full use of the SCADA system to detect\r\nPAGE 6\r\nthe pipe burst location. The system is only used to detect the pipe burst segment is near the pressure measuring\r\npoint according to dramatic change of water pressure at the pressure measuring point, and no effective method\r\nis set up to conduct real time leakage and burst location detecting of the network. In the event of a large burst,\r\nit is still difficult to find the exact location in time. Aiming at these problems, we propose a practical method\r\nbased on SCADA system combining state estimation. This method uses fuzzy similarity priority ratio to detect\r\nthe burst location of urban water supply network in real time.\r\n\r\nReference\r\n[1] Hart, D., Klise, K.A., Bynum, M.L., Laird, C.D. and Seth, A., (2019). Mainly in the last decades, when the monitoring\r\nsystems have become popular and the processing capacity of computer have increased, data mining\r\nand optimization have changed the routine of water companies.\r\nObserving the hydraulic system, measured pressure and flow are the result of a non-linear\r\ncombination of consumed water and leakages, modified by control devices. In signal processing field,\r\nthe leakages and consumers can be read as sources. These sources have different behaviors and their\r\nactions will result in a final pressure and flow signals, acquired by sensors. Bearing it in mind, a set\r\nof techniques for signal separation without previously knowledge of the sources, so called, blind\r\nsource separation (BSS) can be applied on pressure and flow data for leakage detection. Independent\r\nComponent Analysis (ICA), one algorithm from the class of BSS, have been used successfully in\r\nhealth-related research (WESSEL, 2018), seismic activities (CAPUANO et al., 2017), image analysis\r\n(AHMAD et al., 2017), discharge flow separation in water systems (ZHENG et al., 2016). ICA is\r\nable to differentiate a linear combination in a data set, extracting components of source signal without\r\nvariation. (LANGLOIS et al., 2010).\r\nFor the Battle of Leakage Detection and Isolation (BattleDIM), ICA is applied on hydraulic data, for\r\nsource separation helping to identify water consumption and noise sources. The results show that\r\nnoise source is significantly affected by leakages. The second stage of leak detection methodology, a\r\nstatistical controlling method, Abrupt Change Point Detection (CPD), is applied using noise data as\r\ninput. CDP assumes that changes in data affect the statistical properties of the dataset, such as average\r\nand standard deviation. Because of this, in general, CPD techniques monitor changes in some of these\r\nparameters, identifying abrupt changes (LAVIELLE, 2005; BRENTAN et al., 2017).\r\nOptimization processes also gained space in research on WDS, such as in the optimization of pump\r\nand valve control (Mala-Jetmarova, et al, 2017), network calibration (Savic, et al, 2009), etc. Since\r\nBattleDIM offer a non-calibrated model, and pressure/flow dataset, calibration process is applied in\r\ntwo different stages. The first one, a dataset of normal condition is used to calibrate roughness and\r\nbase demand using Particle Swarm Optimization algorithm linked to Epanet hydraulic simulator. The\r\nresults are used on a second stage, where pressure and flow during detected leakages by the ICA-\r\nCDP algorithm is used for calibration of a leakage event. A 24h dataset is used and leakage flow and\r\nnodal leaking position are calibrated. The main objective of this stage is to identify the region of\r\nleaking pipe.\r\nPAGE 2\r\nMETHODOLOGY\r\n\r\nThe methodology used in this work is divided into two parts: Leakage Detection and Leakage\r\nIsolation. The first one is based on data process and signal analysis and has the main goal to identify\r\nwhen leakages start in the network. The second part, Leakage Isolation, is based on optimization\r\nprocess for calibration, and it’s responsible to localize the leakage region.\r\n\r\nLeakage Detection\r\n\r\nFlow and pressure data are used to detect leaks, being separeted in two sources by ICA algorithm.\r\nLanglois et al. (2010) present the fundamentals of the ICA method, which can be denoted through a\r\nrandom vector observed 𝑋= [𝑋1, 𝑋2, … , 𝑋𝑚]𝑇 where 𝑚 elements are mixtures of 𝑚 independent\r\nelements of a random vector 𝑆= [𝑆1, 𝑆2, … , 𝑆𝑚]𝑇 given by:\r\n\r\n𝑋= 𝐴𝑆 (eq. 1)\r\nwhere 𝐴 is a mix matrix 𝑚× 𝑚, the sample value of 𝑋𝑗 is indicated by 𝑥𝑗 and 𝑗= 1,2, … , 𝑚. ICA\r\naims to find an unmixing matrix 𝑊, or the inverse of A, which provides 𝑌, what is the best possible\r\nway to find 𝑆:\r\n\r\n𝑌= 𝑊𝑋≅𝑆 (eq. 2)\r\n\r\nICA is applied for pairs of flow and pressure, generating a large dataset of sources. These sources,\r\nusually, can represent with better precision, anomalies, than mixed signal, mainly when the noise\r\nsource is analyzed. A post-processing methodology is used on the new dataset to identify the start\r\ntime of leakages, the CPD method presented by Lavielle (2005). The algorithm simultaneously\r\ndetermines all change points, given a statistical control parameter, minimizing a penalized contrast\r\nfunction. The contrast function allows to accurately determine the points where statistical parameter\r\ncontrol changes, and the penalty function seeks to reduce the number of points of changes\r\n(BRENTAN et al., 2017). In this study, average and standard deviation values are used as statistical\r\ncontrol parameter.\r\n\r\nLeakage Location\r\n\r\nThe leak location process consists of two steps, the first one calibrates the pipe roughness and nodal\r\ndemands for normal scenario, using the PSO algorithm. The second step uses the results of the\r\ncalibration process to identify the leakage magnitude and region in the network. The sum squared\r\nerror of dimensionless pressure, flow and tank level are used as objective function. A single-objective\r\nproblem F is stated, as presented in equation 3.\r\n\r\n𝐹= ∑\r\n[∑\r\n(\r\n𝑃𝑡,𝑖\r\n𝑚−𝑃𝑡,𝑖\r\n𝑜\r\n𝑃𝑜\r\n̅̅̅̅\r\n)\r\n2\r\n𝑁𝑛\r\n𝑖=1\r\n+ ∑\r\n(\r\n𝑄𝑡,𝑗\r\n𝑚−𝑄𝑡,𝑗\r\n𝑜\r\n𝑄𝑜\r\n̅̅̅̅\r\n)\r\n2\r\n𝑁𝑝\r\n𝑗=1\r\n+ ∑\r\n(\r\n𝐿𝑡,𝑘\r\n𝑚−𝐿𝑡,𝑘\r\n𝑜\r\n𝑇𝑜\r\n̅̅̅̅\r\n)\r\n2\r\n𝑁𝑇\r\n𝑘=1\r\n]\r\n𝑇\r\n𝑡=1\r\n(eq. 3)\r\nwhere 𝑃𝑡,𝑖\r\n𝑚 is the modelled pressure in the node 𝑖 in a network with 𝑁𝑛 monitored nodes, at time\r\nstep 𝑡 in a simulation during 𝑇. 𝑃𝑡,𝑖\r\n𝑜 is the observed pressure for the same node 𝑖 at time step 𝑡.\r\n𝑃𝑜\r\n̅̅̅̅ is the average pressure of observed dataset. 𝑄𝑡,𝑗\r\n𝑚 is the modelled flow in the pipe 𝑗, in a network\r\nwith 𝑁𝑝 monitored pipes, 𝑄𝑡,𝑗\r\n𝑜 is the observed flow in the same pipe and 𝑄𝑜\r\n̅̅̅̅ is the average flow\r\nof observed flow. Finally, 𝐿𝑡,𝑘\r\n𝑚 is the modelled tank level at tank 𝑘 at time step 𝑡 in a network with\r\n𝑁𝑇 monitored tanks, and 𝐿𝑡,𝑘\r\n𝑜 is the observed tank level at the same tank.\r\n\r\nThe fist calibration process has as variables all nodes and all pipes of the water system. PSO algorithm\r\n(EBERHART and KENNEDY, 1995) is applied to minimize the single-objective problem. A swarm\r\nPAGE 3\r\nor population is compound of possible solutions of the problem. In the first iteration of the method\r\nthe particles’ velocity and position are randomly distributed within a range of interest. The objective\r\nfunction is evaluated and the velocity and position are updated. The velocity update is calculated as\r\na linear combination of three components, as presented in the equation (4).\r\n\r\n𝑣𝑖𝑑\r\n𝑛+1 = [𝑤. 𝑣𝑖𝑑\r\n𝑛+\r\n𝑐1.𝑟1(𝑝𝑖𝑑\r\n𝑛−𝑥𝑖𝑑\r\n𝑛)\r\n∆𝑡\r\n+\r\n𝑐2.𝑟2(𝑔𝑖𝑑\r\n𝑛−𝑥𝑖𝑑\r\n𝑛)\r\n∆𝑡\r\n] (eq. 4)\r\n\r\n𝑥𝑖𝑑\r\n𝑛+1 = 𝑥𝑖𝑑\r\n𝑛+ 𝑣𝑖𝑑\r\n𝑛+1. ∆𝑡 (eq. 5)\r\n\r\nwhere 𝑑= 1,2, . . . , 𝐷, is the dimension of decision variable vector, 𝑛= 1,2, . . . , 𝑁 and 𝑁 is the\r\niteration number. 𝑟1 and 𝑟2 are random numbers within the range [0,1]. 𝑃𝑖= (𝑝𝑖1, 𝑝𝑖2, … , 𝑝𝑖𝐷) is\r\nthe best position occupied by a particle and 𝐺𝑖= (𝑔𝑖1, 𝑔𝑖2, … , 𝑔𝑖𝐷) is the best position occupied by\r\nthe swarm. Part of the update is weighted by the cognitive coefficient (𝑐1) social coefficient (𝑐2),\r\nFinally, the velocity 𝑣𝑖𝑑\r\n𝑛 is weighted by the coefficient of inertia (w).\r\n\r\nBATTLEDIM – CASE STUDY\r\nThe methodology is applied to the L-Town network provided in BattLeDIM. The dataset is\r\ncompound by 1 tank water level sensor, 3 flow sensors, 33 pressure sensors and 82 Automated\r\nMetered Readings. These points are monitored in 2018 and 2019 every 5 minutes. For 2018, 10\r\nleakages are fixed, providing for participants the pipe fixed and the time.\r\n\r\nThe application of the ICA is done with different combination of pressure and flow. The choice of\r\npairs (pressure and flow data) follows the division of the network into areas proposed by BattLeDIM,\r\n(e.g areas A, B and C). Area A and B are supplied by two reservoirs, while area C is supplied by a\r\ntank.\r\nThe ICA method resulted, for each application, two sets of components in which they are analyzed\r\nby the CPD technique and several changes points in the signals are indicated. Figure 1 shows the\r\nresults of these applications in one pair of pressre/flow data. The first component follows the demand\r\npattern. The second component has variations in the data directly associated with the leaks.\r\n\r\nFigure 1 – Leak detection - application of ICA and CPD\r\nThe CPD indications are used to built the report file, with the start time for possible leakages in 2019.\r\nPAGE 4\r\nIn BattleDIM’ network, the calibration process demand and roughness are composed by 1687\r\nvariables (782 nodal demands and 905 roughness). One week of pressure, flow and tank level data\r\nare used for the process. Figure 02 shows the comparison of modelled and observed flow in reservoir\r\nR1 and R2.\r\n\r\nFigure 2– Leak Isolation - application of PSO for calibration of nodal demand and pipe roughness.\r\nIt’s possible to verify that leakages increase the outlet flow of reservoirs R1 and R2, making the\r\ndifference between modelled and observed data increase. This fact can be used to estimate the\r\nmagnitude and the start time of the leakages in the network. The black ellipsis highlights the period\r\nused for calibration.\r\nWith the results of the first calibration, the region of leaks is determined, in a second calibration\r\nprocess. A unique and constant leakage flow and node are calibrated. While the simplification of\r\nconstant flow makes the hydraulic simulation softer, this also can affect the precision of the results.\r\nFor the 10 leakages of 2018, the marginal error, calculated as the average Euclidian distance between\r\nthe leaking nodes and the real leaking pipes is around 300m.\r\n\r\nKeywords: Leakage Detection and Localization, Blind Source Separation, Change Point Detection,\r\nCalibration, Particle Swarm Optimization.\r\n\r\nSUMMARY\r\n\r\nLeakage detection and localization is one of the most important challenges of water companies. This\r\nwork presents a multi-step methodology for contributing with discussions in this field. For the Battle\r\nof Leakage Detection and Isolation (BattleDIM), Independent Component Analysis, a blind source\r\nseparation algorithm, is applied on hydraulic data, for source separation helping to identify water\r\nconsumption and noise sources. The results show that noise source is significantly affected by\r\nleakages. In a second stage of leak detection methodology, a statistical controlling method, Abrupt\r\nChange Point Detection (CPD), is applied using noise data as input. CDP assumes that changes in\r\ndata affect the statistical properties of the dataset, such as average and standard deviation. Calibration\r\nprocess is applied in two different stages in order to localize leakages. The first one, a dataset of\r\nnormal condition is used to calibrate roughness and base demand using Particle Swarm Optimization\r\nalgorithm linked to Epanet hydraulic simulator. The results are used on a second stage, where pressure\r\nPAGE 5\r\nand flow during detected leakages by the ICA-CDP algorithm is used for calibration of a leakage\r\nevent. A 24h dataset is used and leakage flow and nodal leaking position are calibrated. The main\r\nobjective of this stage is to identify the region of leaking pipe.\r\n"
  }
]
