BattleDIM Water Network Visualization\r\nPAGE 15\r\nFigure 4: BattleDIM Challenge Water Distribution network with demand measurements in Area C, a\r\nvalve layer without measurements in Area B and sparsely distributed pressure measurements in Area A,\r\ncolours represent the elevation of the edges [6].\r\nPAGE 16\r\nFigure 5: BattleDIM Challenge distribution of nodes with pressure measurement (=red nodes) versus\r\nnodes without pressure measurement (=blue nodes) [6].\r\nFigure 6: BattleDIM Challenge distribution of nodes in Area C with demand measurement (=red nodes)\r\nversus nodes without demand measurement (=blue nodes) [6].\r\n\r\nDOCUMENT: P12b_Dictionary_GSI_2022_conference.txt\r\nPAGE 1\r\nData-driven Leak Localization in Water Distribution Networks via\r\nDictionary Learning and Graph-based Interpolation\r\nPaul Irofti1, Luis Romero-Ben2, Florin Stoican1,3, Vicenc¸ Puig2\r\nAbstract— In this paper, we propose a data-driven leak\r\nlocalization method for water distribution networks (WDNs)\r\nwhich combines two complementary approaches: graph-based\r\ninterpolation and dictionary classification. The former estimates\r\nthe complete WDN hydraulic state (i.e., hydraulic heads) from\r\nreal measurements at certain nodes and the network graph.\r\nThen, we append to the actual measurements a subset of\r\nrelevant estimated states to feed and train the dictionary\r\nlearning scheme. Thus, the meshing of these two methods\r\nis explored, and several promising performance results are\r\nattained, even deriving different mechanisms to increase the\r\nresilience to classical issues (e.g., dimensionality, interpolation\r\nerrors, etc.). The approach is validated using the L-TOWN\r\nbenchmark proposed in the BattLeDIM2020 competition.\r\nIndex Terms— leak localization, dictionary learning, interpo-\r\nlation, water distribution networks.\r\nI. INTRODUCTION\r\nFault detection and isolation (FDI) is an unavoidable\r\nelement in any engineering system that is operated in an\r\nautonomous manner.…677 tokens truncated…, interpolated data (virtual sensors), generated in\r\nan unsupervised way, is added to the real measurements to\r\nfeed the learning step of DL, hence indirectly considering\r\nstructural information during the leak localization. Moreover,\r\nthe use of a robust classification step [8], provided by DL,\r\nallows to improve the node-level classification of GSI.\r\nThus, this article constitutes an initial step in the devel-\r\nopment of a method which combines two separate methods\r\nsuch that it compensates their weaknesses and boosts their\r\nstrengths. Additionally, a new application scheme is pro-\r\nposed to alleviate the drawbacks of introducing approximated\r\ndata to the learning step of DL: separate dictionaries con-\r\nsidering individual extra virtual sensors (subsequently com-\r\nbined using a voting rule) are derived, instead of computing a\r\nsingle dictionary that encompasses all those virtual sensors.\r\nThe theoretical elements are tested over the L-Town\r\nbenchmark proposed in [9], carrying out simulations on\r\nEPANET [10] to generate the required fault scenarios. Initial\r\nresults show that the methodology is promising in terms\r\nof its comparison with the individual methods, settling an\r\nappropriate path for future developments.\r\nII. PRELIMINARIES\r\nWe briefly introduce the theoretical background used in\r\nthe rest of the paper.\r\nA. Dictionary Learning Methods\r\nRecently, we successfully applied dictionary learning tech-\r\nniques to solve WDN fault detection and isolation prob-\r\nlems [7], [11]. The DL problem trains an over-complete base\r\nD ∈Rm×n, also called a dictionary, on a dataset Y ∈\r\nRm×N to produce the s-sparse representations X ∈Rn×N:\r\nmin\r\nD,X\r\n∥Y −DX∥2\r\nF\r\ns.t.\r\n∥xℓ∥0 ≤s, ℓ= 1 : N\r\n∥dj∥= 1, j = 1 : n\r\n(1)\r\nPAGE 2\r\nThe s nonzero entries of a given dataset sample’s repre-\r\nsentation define the dictionary columns, also called atoms,\r\nand their associated coefficients. A thorough description of\r\nthe field is given in [12].\r\nFor water networks, the dataset consists of sensor node\r\npressure measurements where each sample corresponds to a\r\nleak of a given magnitude that occurs in one of the network\r\nnodes. The FDI task consists of finding which node contains\r\nthe leak. Looking at this as a classification problem, where\r\nthe network nodes represent the classes, we can view the\r\ndataset as multiple column blocks corresponding to pressure\r\nmeasurements of leak scenarios occurring in a given node at\r\nvarious magnitudes.\r\nFor DL classification tasks, Label Consistent K-SVD (LC-\r\nKSVD) [13] extends (1) to simultaneously learn the linear\r\nclassifier W based on labels H with an added discriminate\r\nconstraint given by Q that enforces sets of atoms to only be\r\nused by a given class\r\nmin\r\nD,W ,A,X ∥Y −DX∥2\r\nF +α∥H −W X∥2\r\nF +β∥Q−AX∥2\r\nF\r\n(2)\r\nLocating the leaky node from sensor measurements y is a\r\ntwo step process: first we obtain the sparse representations\r\ny ≈Dx by using a greedy algorithm such as OMP [14];\r\nsecondly, we perform linear classification i = arg max W x\r\nto obtain the leaky node1 i. To accommodate large sets\r\nof data, this approach was extended to the online semi-\r\nsupervised setting in [15], adapted and applied to large\r\nWDNs in [7]. In terms of complexity, performing OMP\r\nfor one data-item costs us O(smn) operations and updating\r\nthe dictionary inside LC-KSVD O(sm2N). Training one\r\ndictionary involves K OMP and dictionary update iterations.\r\nB. Interpolation strategies\r\nIn the past, we have used interpolation methods as part of\r\nleak localization schemes [6]. In particular, this interpolation\r\nprocess works over hydraulic head (node pressure + eleva-\r\ntion) data, measured by pressure sensors (cheaper and easier\r\nto install than other metering systems [16]).\r\nThe network structure is considered to be represented by\r\na graph G = (V, E), composed by a set of nodes, i.e., V =\r\nR∪J , that model the reservoirs (R) and junctions (J ) of the\r\nWDN; and a set of edges, i.e., E, representing the pipes of the\r\nnetwork. The proposed interpolation approximates the actual\r\nrelation, given by the Hazen-Williams formula, between the\r\njunctions’ hydraulic heads using the following linear relation:\r\nfi = 1\r\nφi\r\nωif\r\n(3)\r\nwhere f ∈R|V| is the complete graph-state vector, which\r\nencompasses the estimated and known hydraulic head values;\r\nωi stands for the i-th row of the weighted adjacency matrix\r\nΩ∈R|V|×|V|, which encodes the connectivity among nodes\r\nas well as the strength of these connections; and φi =\r\n1Note that while Y contains only sensor node information, the labels in\r\nH help to identify leaky non-sensorized nodes.\r\nP|V|\r\nj=1 ωij denotes the i-th element of the diagonal of the\r\ndegree matrix Φ ∈R|V|×|V|, which is a diagonal matrix.\r\nThe weights in the matrix Ωare derived from the actual\r\npipe lengths: considering pij to be the length of the pipe\r\nconnecting nodes vi and vj (pij\r\n= 0 if they are not\r\nadjacent), then ωij =\r\n1\r\npij if pij ̸= 0, and ωij = 0 otherwise.\r\nThis selection increases the effect of closer neighbors over\r\nthe state of a node.\r\nConsidering the previous approximation, the interpolation\r\nprocedure can be expressed as a quadratic programming\r\nproblem (see [6] for the complete development):\r\nmin\r\nf\r\n1\r\n2\r\n\u0002\r\nf T LΦ−2Lf + αγ2\u0003\r\n(4a)\r\ns.t.\r\nBf ≤1|V| · γ\r\n(4b)\r\nγ > 0\r\n(4c)\r\nSf = f s\r\n(4d)\r\nwhere L stands for the graph Laplacian, B is the incidence\r\nmatrix, S is a diagonal matrix with 1 at the elements\r\nwhose associated node is sensorized (and 0 otherwise), and\r\nf s denotes the measurements vector, including the known\r\nhydraulic heads at the elements corresponding to metered\r\nnodes (and 0 elsewhere). α and γ are auxiliary scalars which\r\ncontrol the relative importance of the cost terms in (4a) and,\r\nrespectively, relax the direction inequality constraint (4b).\r\nThe incidence matrix B deserves some additional expla-\r\nnation. Structural information (node position and elevation,\r\npipe length) is usually available but information about flow\r\ndirection within the pipes is much harder to get, due to the\r\nrare availability of flow meters. A possible way to overcome\r\nthis issue is to compute an approximated incidence matrix\r\nonly using the network structure: the shortest path from each\r\nreservoir to each junction node is calculated, storing the path,\r\ni.e., list of ordered nodes, for every possible combination.\r\nConsidering that each pipe is defined by two nodes, we\r\ncan count, for every stored path, how many times a pipe is\r\ntraversed following each one of the two possible directions,\r\nadding all the counts regarding a certain pipe and a certain\r\ndirection to obtain its final count (note that, at the end of\r\nthe process, each pipe would have two assigned final counts,\r\none per possible direction). Then, the flow direction along a\r\npipe is taken as the direction with the higher final count.\r\nIn terms of complexity, the interpolation procedure re-\r\nduces to solving quadratic problems (4) whose complexity is\r\npolynomial in terms of problem dimension (e.g., solving N\r\nproblems with the interior point method gives O(n3.5N)).\r\nIII. METHODOLOGY\r\nThe methodologies presented in Section II have been\r\nsuccessfully applied to solve the leak localization task, as\r\nillustrated in the provided references. Nevertheless, the per-\r\nformance of these techniques is limited by different aspects:\r\n• The DL approach introduced in Section II-A provides\r\na satisfactory node-level localization, i.e., the leaks are\r\nmostly correctly located at the node where they appear.\r\nHowever, the method does not exploit the structure of\r\nPAGE 3\r\nthe graph during the learning process, missing important\r\ninformation that may improve the performance.\r\n• The GSI technique presented in Section II-B, together\r\nwith the localization strategy proposed in [6], explicitly\r\nuses the graph structure for the sake of the leak isola-\r\ntion. However, the node-level localization precision is\r\nlower, as the method was originally conceived to isolate\r\nan area where the leak is located.\r\nThus, we propose a combination of GSI and DL that maxi-\r\nmizes their benefits, leading to the presented leak localization\r\nmethodology, henceforth referred as GSI-DL (note that the\r\nleak detection phase is not pursued here and is assumed to\r\nbe realized through one of the techniques from the state of\r\nthe art, e.g, by tracking changes in the night consumption\r\n[17]). Then, GSI-DL consists of the next steps:\r\n1) The hydraulic dataset (hydraulic heads at the sen-\r\nsorized nodes) is generated or provided, considering\r\ndifferent leak scenarios (leak location, magnitude, oc-\r\ncurring time, etc.), as well as leak-free historical data.\r\n2) The complete hydraulic state of the network (repre-\r\nsented by the hydraulic heads at the WDN nodes) is\r\nestimated from the measured pressure values by means\r\nof GSI, for both the leak and leak-free scenarios, from\r\nwhich we derive the complete residuals dataset.\r\n3) A subset of nodes is selected to play the role of virtual\r\nsensors, i.e., their interpolated state value is added to\r\nthe information provided by the real sensors, obtaining\r\nan assembled residuals dataset.\r\n4) The DL algorithm is fed with the assembled residuals\r\ndataset, performing the training and obtaining the\r\ncorresponding dictionary and linear classifier.\r\nTo conclude, the aim of the combined GSI-DL method\r\nis to merge the complementary strengths of each individual\r\ncomponent (i.e., GSI supplies additional information for DL)\r\nin order to improve the classification performance.\r\nA. Selection of the virtual nodes\r\nThe interpolation scheme retrieves the complete WDN\r\nstate from a reduced set of known values from the physical\r\nsensors subset Sr ⊂V. However, the introduced approxima-\r\ntions cause differences between the actual hydraulic heads\r\nand the computed states at the unknown nodes, with an\r\nerror distribution that strongly depends on the number and\r\nplacement of sensors because they are the unique source of\r\nhydraulic data.\r\nConsidering the importance of the existence of distinctive\r\nfeatures for each leak, the insertion of estimated data must be\r\ncarefully considered to avoid the inclusion of nodes whose\r\nvalues present high differences between actual and estimated\r\nvalue, between leaky and nominal data, etc., which can\r\nhinder the DL process and reduce the localization accuracy.\r\nThus, a grid search is performed over the set of possible\r\nvirtual sensors subsets Sv via training and testing to see if\r\naccuracy improves significantly on the training data. At the\r\nend, we select the best virtual sensors in terms of accuracy.\r\nIn this way, the selected virtual sensors are very likely to add\r\nvaluable information to the learning process and to increase\r\nseparability among leaks.\r\nB. GSI-DL procedure\r\nTo perform dictionary learning, the first step consists on\r\ncollecting readings from pressure sensors in a wide variety of\r\nscenarios (as training samples are required): nominal/leaky,\r\nas well as different leak sizes and locations. This task may\r\nbe solved in two different ways:\r\n• On the one hand, synthetic sensor data can be generated\r\nfrom a known hydraulic model. For a better perfor-\r\nmance assessment, we use this approach for this article.\r\n• On the other hand, if the network is clustered to define\r\ngroups of nodes whose leak behaviour is similar, a set\r\nof leak experiments can be performed together with the\r\nwater utility at the real WDN to gather the sensor data.\r\nIn our case, considering the typically large size of real\r\nnetworks, as well as the computational cost of performing\r\nsimulations for every possible leak event, a subset Z ⊆V\r\nof nodes is chosen so that |Z| = c ≤N leak sources (labels\r\nfor training) are considered, running scenarios with various\r\nfault magnitudes for each node zi. This subset of nodes must\r\nbe scattered throughout the network to capture its hydraulic\r\nbehaviour in the most complete way.\r\nNoting that M different leak sizes are considered for\r\neach possible leak location (t time instants are computed\r\nconsidering each leak size), and that the number of physical\r\nsensors of the network is |Sr|, the complete data set ˜\r\nFleak ∈\r\nR|Sr|×ψ, with ψ = cMt, may be regarded as the union of\r\nc blocks corresponding to each faulty node zi, where each\r\nblock contains Mt samples with M different leak sizes.\r\nMoreover, a complementary leak-free/nominal dataset\r\n˜\r\nFnom ∈R|Sr|×ψ is required: each column ˜\r\nfnom must be\r\nobtained for similar boundary conditions at the WDN to the\r\ncase of its analogue (by position) column ˜\r\nfleak from ˜\r\nFleak.\r\nIn this way, the necessary residuals for DL are obtained while\r\nsimultaneously reducing the differences between leak and\r\nleak-free scenarios that are not caused by the leak.\r\nThe achieved datasets must be divided into training and\r\ntesting sets. Then, the learning process, explained in Section\r\nII-A, is applied as summarized by Algorithm 1 (consider that\r\nsrv = |Sr| + |Sv| is the total number of sensors).\r\nThe obtained matrices, i.e., D and W , are then used to\r\nclassify the entries of the testing dataset in order to assess\r\nthe reliability of the solution. Algorithm 2 summarizes the\r\nbehavior of the classifier against new data entries.\r\nC. GSI-DL with multiple dictionaries\r\nThe application of the strategy is limited due to the inser-\r\ntion of data from multiple interpolated nodes, which leads\r\nto an accumulation of approximation errors and, above a\r\nthreshold, deteriorates the leak localization efficacy. Thus, we\r\ndevise a new scheme to overcome the above where, instead of\r\nlearning a single dictionary-classifier pair that is trained with\r\nthe entire set of additional virtual sensor information, we\r\nlearn multiple dictionaries (MD)-classifiers pairs by applying\r\nAlgorithm 1 separately for each individual virtual sensor.\r\nPAGE 4\r\nAlgorithm 1: GSI-DL — Training procedure\r\nData: leak training set ˜\r\nF train\r\nleak\r\n∈R|Sr|×ψtr, nominal\r\ntraining set ˜\r\nF train\r\nnom ∈R|Sr|×ψtr, sparsity level\r\ns ∈R, virtual sensors Sv, physical sensors Sr\r\nResult: dictionary D ∈Rsrv×n, classifier W ∈Rc×n\r\n1 interpolate F train\r\nnom\r\napplying (4) to ˜\r\nF train\r\nnom\r\n2 interpolate F train\r\nleak\r\napplying (4) to ˜\r\nF train\r\nleak\r\n3 compute residuals: Y train = F train\r\nnom −F train\r\nleak\r\n4 assemble dataset (and associated labels Htrain\r\nrv\r\n):\r\nY train\r\nrv\r\n=\r\n\u0002\r\nY train(Sr); Y train(Sv)\r\n\u0003\r\n5 compute D, W , A applying (2) to labeled Y train\r\nrv\r\nAlgorithm 2: GSI-DL — Classification procedure\r\nData: sample ˜\r\nf test\r\nleak ∈R|Sr|, nominal sample\r\n˜\r\nf test\r\nnom ∈R|Sr|, sparsity level s ∈R, virtual\r\nsensors Sv, physical sensors Sr, dictionary\r\nD ∈Rsrv×n, classifier W ∈Rc×n\r\nResult: faulty node i\r\n1 interpolate f test\r\nnom applying (4) to ˜\r\nf test\r\nnom\r\n2 interpolate f test\r\nleak applying (4) to ˜\r\nf test\r\nleak\r\n3 compute residuals sample: ytest = f test\r\nnom −f test\r\nleak\r\n4 assemble data: ytest\r\nrv\r\n=\r\n\u0002\r\nytest(Sr); ytest(Sv)\r\n\u0003\r\n5 representation: x = OMP(ytest\r\nrv , D, s)\r\n6 classification: i = arg maxj=1:c(W x)\r\nThe result consists of |Sv| dictionaries-classifiers pairs, each\r\none trained with a single additional virtual sensor.\r\nFor the final localization decision, we employ a voting\r\nmethod [18] on the set of classifications from the dictionary-\r\nclassifier pairs. Several advantages are derived from the ap-\r\nplication of this scheme, chiefly, a more robust classification\r\nresult [8] and a reduction in the outliers’ effect.\r\nIV. RESULTS\r\nThe presented methodologies are implemented in a realis-\r\ntic case study, provided by the “Battle of Leakage Detection\r\nand Isolation Methods 2020” (BattLeDIM2020), detailed\r\nin [9]. This benchmark, illustrated in Fig.1, consists of a\r\nsmall hypothetical network composed of 782 inner nodes,\r\n909 pipes, 1 tank and 2 water inlets or reservoirs. The\r\nnetwork is composed from three distinct areas (A, B and\r\nC), distinguished by the elevation of its nodes. Hereinafter,\r\nwe focus on area A.\r\nA. Data generation\r\nFor testing purposes, hydraulic data must be available for\r\nall the considered leaks. In this article, this information is\r\nretrieved through the simulation of an EPANET model.\r\nA list Z ⊂V of 30 nodes is selected from the junction\r\nnodes (denoted in Fig.1 with “filled blue circle” symbols) as\r\npossible leak sources (“red cross” symbols). Henceforth, 30\r\nzone B\r\nzone C\r\njunction node\r\nreservoir node\r\ntank node\r\nsensor\r\nvirtual sensor\r\nnode under fault\r\nlegend\r\nFig. 1.\r\nL-Town illustration with various node highlights\r\nlabels (one per each leak) are considered in the subsequent\r\nlearning steps. For each possible leak, a week of hydraulic\r\ndata is generated, with a time step of 5 minutes. The first\r\nfour days of the week are selected to generate the training\r\nset, increasing the leak size each day to cover a wide range\r\nof values, from 1 m3/h to 7 m3/h. The three remaining days\r\nproduce the testing set, selecting even leak sizes between the\r\nminimum and maximum values to complete the set, i.e., 2,\r\n4 and 6 m3/h. Finally, a 5% of uncertainty is induced in the\r\npipe roughness and diameters, whereas the demand patterns\r\nare kept as provided by the BattLeDIM2020 case.\r\nB. Application of preliminaries\r\nFirst, the preliminary works in Section II, i.e., the sepa-\r\nrated application of DL [7] and GSI (together with a leak\r\ncandidate selection method: GSI-LCSM) [6]; are applied to\r\nthe case study. This is done to provide performance baselines\r\nand to justify the interest in merging the procedures.\r\nOn the one hand, the classical DL approach is trained\r\nwith the measurements from the real sensors, obtaining\r\nan accuracy of 89.63%, which can be considered as the\r\nreference value to improve with the new proposed schemes\r\nthat include the interpolated information. This accuracy is\r\nincluded in Table I together with the rest of the single-\r\ndictionary results, as they are directly comparable.\r\nOn the other hand, GSI-LCSM is fully data-driven (it\r\ndoes not require prior leak information) and is conceived\r\nto provide a candidate localization area, which limits the\r\nprecision for single node localization. Thus, we need to select\r\na suitable criteria to determine the success of the localization\r\nprocess, in order to perform a comparable analysis with\r\nrespect to the DL ones. Let us consider that GSI-LCSM\r\nprovides node vG as the best candidate. If we only accept\r\nlocalization results as successful when the distance from vG\r\nto vl (node of the actual leak), i.e, r(vG, vl); is the minimum\r\namong the distances from vG to all the possible leaks, a\r\nreduced accuracy of 56.7% is achieved. However, this criteria\r\ncan be extended to also consider the case when vl is not\r\nthe closest to vG, but the 2nd-nearest one (let us denote\r\nthe closest one as vc). Accepting only the concrete cases\r\nwhen r(vG, vl)−r(vG, vc) < 100 (meters), the accuracy is\r\ndrastically increased, reaching a 76.7%. Finally, an accuracy\r\n"
  }
]

CONCLUSIONS\r\nThis article proposes a method for leak localization in\r\nWDNs that combines graph-based state interpolation (GSI)\r\nand dictionary learning (DL). We have presented the original\r\nmethods, explaining advantages and drawbacks to show and\r\njustify the interest in their complementary application.\r\nFurthermore, several different schemes have been intro-\r\nduced regarding this combination, i.e., computing a single\r\ndictionary that considers a set of interpolated values, deriving\r\nmultiple dictionaries (classical or including virtual sensors)\r\nand voting over their classification results, etc.\r\nThe advantages and limitations of all these approaches\r\nhave been stated and demonstrated by means of a case study,\r\nbased on the L-Town benchmark from BattLeDIM2020,\r\nobtaining several promising results.\r\nFurther works will cover additional enhancements of the\r\nmethodology, such as a better integration of the components,\r\nan analysis of the interpolation procedure to improve its\r\nperformance (weight computation, nonlinear fitting, etc.).\r\n\r\nDOCUMENT: P03_Pressure_leak_duality_2022.txt\r\nPAGE 5\r\ncorrelation-based method similar to Sanz et al. (2016). In cases\r\nwith multiple leaks that appear simultaneously, the leaks are local-\r\nized one by one, eliminated from the dual model, and the remaining\r\nleaks are detected and located subsequently through an iterative\r\napproach.\r\nCalibration\r\nNodal Demand Calibration\r\nThe AMRs data are used to develop a demand model through\r\nTSA for the unmeasured customers within the network. Various\r\ntime series models (Shumway and Stoffer 2010) are tested on the\r\nAMRs, aiming to extract weekly seasonalities and yearly trends for\r\ndifferent customer types (e.g., residential, commercial). The best\r\nperformance is achieved with a rather simple model, consisting of\r\na multiplicative superposition of weekly seasonalities (SðtÞ), a\r\ntime-varying trend (TðtÞ), and a random component (RðtÞ) account-\r\ning for stochastic variations and measurement noise\r\ndðtÞ ¼ ¯d · TðtÞ · SðtÞ · RðtÞ\r\nð1Þ\r\nwith ¯d = customer’s base demand. For each AMR time series, the\r\ntrend component TðtÞ is estimated using a convolution filter and\r\nsubsequently removed by dividing the original time series through\r\nTðtÞ, followed by estimation of SðtÞ through periodic averages over\r\nthe trend-free series (Seabold and Perktold 2010). After removing\r\nthe seasonal component by dividing the trend-free series by SðtÞ,\r\nonly the random component RðtÞ remains. Subsequently, similar-\r\nities in the individual seasonal patterns are identified through time\r\nseries clustering (Steffelbauer et al. 2021). Furthermore, cluster\r\nanalysis is used to identify the number of distinct patterns nd\r\nand outliers. For each demand node i of the network model, a\r\ntime-varying demand time series ˆdi is built as a superposition of\r\nthe distinct patterns weighted by their individual averages ¯dij as-\r\nsociated with the patterns\r\nˆdiðtÞ ¼\r\nX\r\nnd\r\nj¼1\r\n¯dij · TjðtÞ · SjðtÞ\r\nð2Þ\r\nThe random time series components are neglected when build-\r\ning the estimates ˆdi.\r\nPipe Roughness Calibration\r\nPipes with the same material, age, diameter, hydraulic conditions,\r\nand locations are grouped in clusters with the same roughness value\r\n[in this case a Hazen-Williams (HW) coefficient]\r\nCHW ¼ MHWx\r\nð3Þ\r\nwhere MHW is the membership matrix of the np pipes to nc clusters\r\nof HW coefficients; x ∈Rnc is the vector of roughness cluster\r\nvalues to calibrate; and CHW ∈Rnp is the vector of HW coefficients\r\nof pipes. Roughness calibration aims to fit the measurements by\r\nadjusting the roughness coefficients of the hydraulic model. The\r\nfollowing nonlinear regression equations need to be considered:\r\n½zj\u0002i ¼ ½Syðtj; xÞ\u0002i þ εij\r\nð4Þ\r\nwhere yðtj; xÞ = hydraulic state that is implicitly defined by the\r\nextended period simulations at time tj; zj ∈Rnm is the vector of\r\nmeasurements at time tj; S is the selection matrix to select state\r\nvectors that correspond to the measurements; and εij ∼N ð0; σ2\r\nijÞ\r\nare independent and identically distributed Gaussian error terms\r\nwith zero expectation and standard deviation σij.\r\nThe box-constrained WLS problem for parameter calibration\r\nconsists of seeking to minimize the differentiable criterion\r\nmin\r\nxL≤x≤xUfðxÞ ≜1\r\n2\r\nX\r\nnt\r\nj¼1\r\nX\r\nnm\r\ni¼1\r\nHκ\r\n\u0002½Syðtj; xÞ\u0002i −½zj\u0002i\r\nσij\r\n\u0003\r\nþ α\r\n2 kx −x0k2\r\n2\r\nð5Þ\r\nwhere in place of the traditional least-squares criterion the weighted\r\nHuber function Hκ with parameter κ is used, as in Preis et al.\r\n(2011), to increase the robustness of parameter estimates against\r\noutliers; nt = number of observation times; nm = number of mea-\r\nsurements; xL and xU = lower and upper bounds; x0 = prior infor-\r\nmation about x (e.g., initial value in the EPANET file); and α =\r\nTikhonov regularization coefficient, which penalizes large depar-\r\ntures from x0 for sufficiently large α and increases the robustness\r\nof parameter estimates against outliers. The state-of-the-art algo-\r\nrithm for solving a differentiable WLS problem is the iterative\r\nLevenberg-Marquardt algorithm. At each iteration step, the gra-\r\ndient of f is calculated to estimate the Hessian matrix at the last\r\nestimate xk. The gradient of f at xk is\r\n∇fk ¼\r\nX\r\nnt\r\nj¼1\r\nJðtj; xkÞTWj ~Rðtj; xkÞ þ αðxk −x0Þ\r\nð6Þ\r\nwhere Wj is the diagonal weight matrix at time tj; Jðtj; xkÞ ¼\r\nS∂xyðtj; xkÞ is the Jacobian matrix of the prediction function at xk,\r\nwith ∂xy using the postmultiplication by P ¼ MHW as in Piller\r\net al. In the DA, the\r\nmodel is augmented with ns virtual reservoirs that are connected\r\nwith pressure measurement nodes by valves. The origin of the name\r\ndual stems from the fact that, instead of using the fixed-demand\r\nboundary condition at the sensor nodes (i.e., the original or primal\r\nhydraulic model), the measured pressure heads are used as fixed-\r\nhead boundary conditions at the corresponding virtual reservoirs.\r\nConsequently, the heads at the measurement nodes become free\r\nvariables and imbalances in the system compared to a leak-free\r\nmodel lead to flows to the virtual reservoirs. If there are no leaks,\r\nand if we set the minor loss of each virtual reservoir’s valve to\r\nzero, the two networks are equivalent. In the hydraulic model, we\r\nnormally set these valves’ minor loss to a sufficiently low but\r\nnonzero value, and so the primal and dual networks are numerically\r\nequivalent but not mathematically equivalent.\r\nIf a new leak appears in the primal model, the residuals between\r\nmeasured and calculated pressures change. The pressure drops\r\ncaused by higher flow velocities toward the leak in the real system\r\nare not observed in the model that is still based on the leak-free\r\nsystem. In the dual approach, the measured pressure drop is applied\r\nto the fixed-head reservoirs and, as a consequence, an additional\r\noutflow is generated. This outflow can be understood as an outflow\r\nresidual or virtual leak flow. The advantage of the DA is that the\r\ncalculated outflows act as amplifiers that deliver significant and\r\nlocalized signals even for small pressure drops. In addition, the out-\r\nflows at the virtual reservoirs serve a good first estimate for the\r\nleak’s size and location.\r\nDual Model Sensitivities\r\nWe consider the dual WDN with np pipes, ns virtual links, and nj\r\njunction nodes at which the heads are unknown. We also denote\r\nthe vector of unknown flows in the pipes and virtual links by\r\nq ∈Rnpþns, and the unknown heads and demands at the (free) no-\r\ndes by h ∈Rnj and d ∈Rnj, respectively. The sensitivities of heads\r\nand pipe flow rates with respect to nodal outflows are derived\r\namong other sensitivities in Piller et al. (2017). The local sensitiv-\r\nities ∇dh and ∇dq can be calculated in demand-driven analysis as\r\nfollows:\r\n∇dh ¼ −ðATF−1AÞ−1\r\n∇dq ¼ −F−1AðATF−1AÞ−1\r\nð13Þ\r\nwhere A is the link-node-incidence matrix of the dual network\r\ngraph reduced to junction nodes (all links, including pipes and\r\nCluster\r\n1\r\n3\r\n2\r\n4\r\n5\r\n6\r\n(b)\r\n(c)\r\n(d)\r\n90\r\n100\r\n110\r\n120\r\n130\r\n140\r\n150\r\n160\r\n0\r\n2\r\n4\r\n6\r\n8\r\n10\r\n12\r\n14\r\n16\r\nCHW\r\n# Iterations\r\nCluster 1\r\nCluster 2\r\nCluster 3\r\nCluster 4\r\nCluster 5\r\nCluster 6\r\n(a)\r\nFig. 2. Network colored by calibration clusters of Hazen-Williams roughness coefficients. Pressure measurements are shown as circles. (a) Roughness\r\niterations; (b) the principle of the dual model, where the pressure measurements are replaced by the combination of a valve and a virtual reservoir\r\nwhose head is equal to the measured head hi, and the dual model transforms hi into virtual leakage flows qvi; (c) pressure residuals for the first week of\r\n2018; and (d) the minimum, maximum, and root-mean-square errors (RMSEs) in increasing RMSE order.\r\n© ASCE\r\n04021106-4\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(3): 04021106\r\nDownloaded from ascelibrary.org by Technische Universiteit Delft on 01/25/22. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\r\nPAGE 7\r\nvirtual links, are taken); and F is the diagonal matrix of head loss\r\nderivatives with respect to q.\r\nLet Af ∈RðnpþnsÞ×ðnfþnsÞ be the link-node-incidence matrix of\r\nthe dual network graph reduced to fixed-head nodes (the nf initial\r\ntanks and reservoirs, and the ns virtual reservoirs), and let qin ¼\r\nAT\r\nf q represent the unknown flow rate entering in the system\r\n(leaving the fixed-head nodes if positive). Then the sensitivity\r\nof the qin can be written as using Eq. (13)\r\n∇dqin ¼ −AT\r\nf F−1AðATF−1AÞ−1\r\nð14Þ\r\nThe Jacobian matrix in Eq. (14) is the matrix of first-order deriv-\r\natives of the inflows calculated at virtual pressure nodes at meas-\r\nurement locations and real pressure boundary conditions such as\r\nreservoirs. The ði; jÞ element of ∇dqin represents the first-order\r\nchange rate of the calculated in- or outflow at a fixed-head node\r\ni as a consequence of a change in demand at node j.\r\nIn the dual model, the in- and outflows at the virtual reservoir are\r\nan indicator for a real existing leak or model errors. In a perfect\r\nmodel, where all the parameters are known, the calculated pres-\r\nsures of the dual model would be exactly the same as the measure-\r\nments from a primal model. In the corresponding dual model,\r\nthe calculated in- and outflows at junctions would be zero and\r\nthe primal and dual models would give approximately the same\r\nresults (i.e., except for small numerical differences due to the minor\r\nlosses across the virtual reservoir valves).\r\nIn the presence of an unknown leak, the measured pressure\r\nheads and the values calculated by the leak-free primal model di-\r\nverge. In the dual model, the pressures at the measurement nodes\r\nbecome free and the measurements are set as virtual fixed heads\r\n[Fig. 2(b)]. The imbalance caused by the unknown leak is then\r\nexpressed as in- and outflows calculated at pressure measurement\r\nnodes. However, as shown in the BattLeDIM (Steffelbauer et al.\r\n2020), the sensitivity is much higher in the dual model. Inverting\r\nthe problem acts as an amplifier of leaks. Another advantage is that\r\nthe imbalances and the value in question (leaks) have the same unit\r\nof flow. The sum of all the imbalances normally gives a good first\r\nestimate of the size of the leak. For explanation of the amplifying\r\neffect, a deeper investigation of Eq. (14) may be useful: from the\r\nbalance of inflows and outflows, it is possible to deduce each\r\ncolumn of ∇dqin including the fraction of in- and outflows as a\r\nresponse to the change in outflow at the corresponding demand\r\nnode equation\r\n1T\r\nnfþnsqin ¼ 1Tnjd ⇒1T\r\nnfþns∇dqin ¼ 1Tnj\r\nð15Þ\r\nThe sum of the column vector must be 1. Naturally, the response\r\nshould be an inflow for all fixed-head nodes.\r\nLeak Detection and Localization\r\nLeak Detection with the Dual Model\r\nWhereas in the past, human operators were in charge of small\r\nsingle-supply areas, modern WU employees are responsible for\r\nmultiple DMAs simultaneously (Bakker et al. 2014). That is why\r\nautomatic anomaly detection algorithms are of particular interest\r\nfor providing a rapid response to leaks and burst pipes (Romano\r\net al. 2013). However, a correct estimation of the total leakage out-\r\nflow over their time of existence (from the start tS until the end tE\r\nwhen they are repaired) is of utmost importance to assess water\r\nlosses (Hamilton and McKenzie 2014). The correct identification\r\nof tS is also one of the objectives in the BattLeDIM (Vrachimis\r\net al. 2020). We developed a two-stage approach to tackle both\r\ntasks: (1) using anomaly detection algorithms to detect leaks as\r\nquickly as possible, and (2) using regression analysis to retrieve\r\ngood leak start time tS estimates. For both approaches the virtual\r\nleak flows ½qv\u0002i ¼ −½qin\u0002iþnf (the dual model’s outflows to the\r\nvirtual reservoirs) are used (Fig. 4, for example).\r\nTwo algorithms were used to detect leaks in the qv: (1) the\r\nCUSUM algorithm, where a a leak is detected when the cumulative\r\nsum of positive and negative differences in the signal exceeds a\r\ncertain threshold τ 1, and (2) the likelihood ratio test (Basseville\r\nand Nikiforov 1993), where a leak is detected if the ratio between\r\nthe likelihood of the leak versus the leak-free case exceeds a certain\r\nthreshold τ2. The ideal thresholds for both methods are obtained\r\nthrough calibration on leak-free data.\r\nVisual inspection of the virtual leakage outflows qv of detected\r\nleaks revealed two different types of leaks. The first leak type TI is\r\na sudden pipe burst that happens instantaneously at tS\r\nqLðtÞ ¼\r\n\u0004 0\r\nfor t < tS\r\nqS\r\nfor t ≥tS\r\nð16Þ\r\nwhere qLðtÞ = leakage outflow over time; and qS = saturated (maxi-\r\nmum) leak flow (e.g., Leak 3 in Fig. 4). Leaks are not modeled as\r\npressure-dependent demands in contrast to the leaks generated in\r\nthe BattLeDIM. The second leak type TII is a slowly growing leak\r\nstarting at tS and saturating at a certain time tSA, modeled as a piece-\r\nwise function with a quadratic growth rate before the saturation\r\n(e.g., Leaks 1, 2, and 4 in Fig. 4)\r\nqLðtÞ ¼\r\n8\r\n>\r\n>\r\n<\r\n>\r\n>\r\n:\r\n0\r\nfor t < tS\r\na · t2 þ b · t þ c\r\nfor tS ≤t ≤tSA\r\nqS\r\nfor t > tSA\r\nð17Þ\r\nThe coefficients of the quadratic outflow model connect the\r\ncurves through following relationships a ¼ ðqS −bðtSA −tSÞ=\r\nðt2\r\nSA −t2\r\nSÞ and c ¼ −at2\r\nS −btS. Additionally, it was found that\r\nleaks are evolving simultaneously in the system, which makes de-\r\ntection more difficult. If a single leak evolves over time, a Bayesian\r\ninference approach based on Hamiltonian Monte Carlo algorithm\r\n(Hoffman and Gelman 2014) is used (e.g., in Area C) to identify the\r\nparameters tS; tSA; qS; a; b, and c plus the confidence intervals of\r\nthe leak model parameters. In the case of multiple evolving leaks\r\n(Area A&B), differential evolution is used to identify the best com-\r\nbination of leak outflows over time plus the leak parameters of each\r\nsingle leak (Storn and Price 1997). The identified leak outflows\r\nwere compared against the outcomes of the DA and subsequently\r\nused for the leak localization.\r\nLeak Localization with the Dual Model\r\nThe Pearson correlation for flow and pressure residuals and the\r\nfirst-order estimates using sensitivities are calculated for the locali-\r\nzation (Perez et al. 2014). It is more convenient for implementation\r\npurposes to work with the pressure residuals and sensitivities of the\r\noriginal measurement nodes instead of using the inflow sensitivities\r\nin Eq. (14) (e.g., no need for calculating Af and changing the set of\r\nvariable pressure nodes). This does not affect the main idea because\r\nthe sensitivity of the head is equivalent to the head loss of the virtual\r\nvalve and hence proportional to the flow sensitivity in the linearized\r\nsystem.\r\nThe vector of the sensitivities of measured head is deter-\r\nmined by\r\n∇dhm ¼ −SðATF−1AÞ−1\r\nð18Þ\r\nThe term S is the same selection matrix for the measurement\r\nnodes as in Eq. (4).\r\n© ASCE\r\n04021106-5\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(3): 04021106\r\nDownloaded from ascelibrary.org by Technische Universiteit Delft on 01/25/22. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\r\nPAGE 8\r\nThe difference between Eqs. (18) and (14) consists in the multi-\r\nplication by the derivative of the valve head loss: (½Sh\u0002i −hf\r\nnfþi ¼\r\nKij½qv\u0002ij½qv\u0002i ⇒∂djð½Sh\u0002iÞ ¼ −2Kij½qv\u0002ij∂djð½qin\u0002nfþiÞ).\r\nIf\r\nthe\r\nsensitivities following Eq. (18) are used, the pressure residuals\r\nare used for the calculation of the correlation, whereas the simu-\r\nlated external flows at the virtual reservoirs are considered in\r\nthe case of Eq. This adjustment\r\neliminates the noise from the pressure measurements and stabilizes\r\nthe calculated set of candidates for the unknown leak. The Pearson\r\ncorrelation ρr;Sð·;iÞ is calculated as\r\nρr;Sð·;iÞ ¼ covðr; Sð·;iÞÞ\r\nσr · σSð·;iÞ\r\nð19Þ\r\nwhere r is the vector of residuals; Sð·;iÞ is the sensitivity vector of\r\nnode i; covð·Þ is the covariance; and σr and σSð·;iÞ = standard devia-\r\ntions of the residual vector and the sensitivity vector, respectively.\r\nThe residuals and the sensitivity coefficients are very small. How-\r\never, this did not show any negative impact in the allocation in our\r\ntests. In contrast, the system is stabilized by the additional pressure\r\nboundary conditions, which makes the correlation more stable\r\ncompared to the conventional primal model approach. One impor-\r\ntant limitation of the correlation method is that it does not work for\r\ntwo or more leaks appearing at the same time. Therefore, a single\r\nleak must first be isolated in time from other leaks in order to be\r\nlocalized. The leakage curves that have been calculated for detec-\r\ntion serve as a basis for choosing the best time for allocation, and\r\nwe use a step-by-step procedure for localizing simultaneously\r\ngrowing leaks:\r\n1. Identify the time interval that starts briefly before the new un-\r\nknown leak starts and ends before the next leak starts. The time\r\nintervals from tS to tSA are found by a combination of CUSUM\r\nor likelihood ratio tests with Hamilton Monte Carlo or differ-\r\nential evolution (depending on the single- or multiple-leak case)\r\nas described in the leak detection paragraph in the “Methods”\r\nsection.\r\n2. Initialize calculation for the selected time interval (load all\r\nmeasurements as well as the estimated demands).\r\n3. Run extended-period simulations for selected time interval; for\r\neach time step,\r\na. Update boundary conditions via toolkit functions including\r\ndemand patterns, heads at virtual reservoirs, pump flow;\r\nand\r\nb. Update all known leaks with their calculated leak flows as\r\nfixed demands and define the start time of the unknown leak\r\nbased on the results of the detection.\r\n4. Simulate the time step (here the EPANET toolkit is used)\r\nand after each time step with active new unknown leak, cal-\r\nculate correlation in Eq. (19) for all nodes based on the\r\nsensitivities.\r\n5. Consider only the nodes with a correlation score higher than\r\na given minimum threshold (e.g., 0.95) and add those eligible\r\ncorrelations to the sum of correlation taken over all calculated\r\ntime steps.\r\n6. Identify the node with the highest correlation sum as the can-\r\ndidate for the new leak within this time interval.\r\n7. Add the new leak to the list of known leaks and the leakage flow\r\nis considered as a known demand for the localization of the next\r\nleak and the procedure is repeated from Step 1 until all leaks\r\nhave been identified in the given period.\r\nL-Town Case Study and Measurement Data\r\nThe case study network L-Town was provided by the organizers of\r\nthe BattLeDIM (Vrachimis et al. 2020). L-Town is a small hypo-\r\nthetical town based on a real WDN in Cyprus with approximately\r\n10,000 inhabitants, who receive water from two reservoirs. The\r\nWDN consists of pipes with diameters ranging from 63 to 225 mm\r\nand a total pipe length of 43 km. L-Town consists of three distinct\r\nhydraulic areas: (1) Area A is the main part of the network, (2) Area\r\nB is a low-lying part that is supplied through a pressure reduction\r\nvalve, and (3) Area C is an area with higher elevation that is sup-\r\nplied by an elevated tank fed from Area A through a pumping sta-\r\ntion. An overview of the network and the location of the three\r\nmeasurement zones can be found in Fig. 2.\r\nTo enhance the water loss monitoring capabilities, the WU of\r\nL-Town installed three flowmeters (two at the reservoirs and one\r\nat the pumping station), a tank-level sensor, and 33 pressure sensors\r\n(depicted as circles in Fig. 2). All sensors measure and transmit data\r\nevery 5 min to the utility’s supervisory control and data acquisition\r\n(SCADA) system. Additionally, the WU installed 82 smart water\r\nmeters or AMRs in Area C, measuring three different customer\r\ntypes: residential, commercial, and industrial. There is no flow-\r\nmeter installed at the tank that feeds Area C. Therefore, a virtual\r\ninflow measurement to Area C has to be reconstructed from the\r\ntank-level measurements and the inflow measurement measured at\r\nthe pump that supplies the tank.\r\nThe data set of the BattLeDIM contains 2 years of sensor data\r\nfor 2018 (historical data set) and 2019 (validation data set), an\r\nEPANET model of the water distribution network, plus the time\r\nand repair location of 10 pipe bursts that were fixed in 2018. Three\r\ntypes of leaks exist: (1) small background leaks with 1%–5% of the\r\naverage inflow, (2) medium pipe breaks with 5%–10%, and\r\n(3) large pipe bursts with leakage flows of more than 10% of\r\nthe average system inflow (≈180 m3=h). Large leakages with out-\r\nflows over 15 m3=h are fixed by the water utility after a reasonable\r\namount of time within 2 months. The leakages have two different\r\ntime profiles: either (1) abrupt pipe bursts with constant leak flow\r\nrates, or (2) background leakages with growing leak rates that\r\nevolve over time until large outflow rates at which they remain con-\r\nstant. In total, 14 leakages occurred in 2018 with outflow rates be-\r\ntween 5 and 35 m3=h, of which 10 leaks have been repaired. The\r\nremaining four leaks are not repaired and continue into the 2019\r\nvalidation data set. The BattLeDIM challenge is to find the 19 leaks\r\nthat happened in 2019 plus the four remaining leaks. The outflows\r\nand locations of the 33 leaks can be found in Figs. 6–9 (dashed lines\r\nin the outflow time series plots and circles in the location overview\r\nplots). More details on the data set can be found in Vrachimis\r\net al. (2020).\r\nResults and Discussion\r\nDemand Calibration\r\nEach AMR time series is decomposed into its trend, seasonal (with\r\na period length of a week), and random components using the\r\nmultiplicative time series model described in Eq. (1). Subsequently,\r\ncluster analysis is used to identify similarities in the trend and\r\nseasonal patterns. Two distinct demand patterns emerge in the trend\r\nTðtÞ and in the seasonal components SðtÞ, residential (TRðtÞ;\r\nSRðtÞ) and commercial (TCðtÞ; SCðtÞ). The seasonal and trend com-\r\nponents are shown in Fig. 3 for each AMR measurement. Further-\r\nmore, some patterns are found to be a superposition of both pattern\r\ntypes. These patterns belong to houses with mixed user groups\r\n(e.g., commercial space in the ground floor and apartments in\r\n© ASCE\r\n04021106-6\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(3): 04021106\r\nDownloaded from ascelibrary.org by Technische Universiteit Delft on 01/25/22. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\r\nPAGE 9\r\nthe floors above). Subsequently, these patterns are called mixed pat-\r\nterns. Generally, all demand patterns can be described through the\r\nsuperposition [Eq. (2)] of the residential and commercial pattern.\r\nDuring workdays (Monday to Friday), water consumption follows\r\na similar behavior, whereas during the weekend (Saturday and\r\nSunday) higher consumption during late hours occurs as the result\r\nof night life [Fig. 3(a)]. Furthermore, cluster analysis revealed four\r\noutlier patterns in the AMR measurements. After closer examina-\r\ntion, these outlier patterns were explained as industrial users with a\r\nperiodicity differing from a week (i.e., 9, 11, or 13 days). Hence,\r\nthose industrial users do not follow the same pattern of consump-\r\ntion as described in Eq. (2) and are not further used in the demand\r\nmodeling. The trend components in Fig. 3(b) show higher water\r\nusage during July and August, and lower usage in December\r\nand January.\r\nThe demand model is used to model the unmeasured cus-\r\ntomers within the L-Town network. Additionally, a virtual inflow\r\nmeasurement of Area C was constructed from the pump flow mea-\r\nsurements and the tank’s water level. This virtual inflow is used to\r\n(1) validate the demand model; and (2) estimate the leak outflow in\r\nArea C. Fig. 4(a) shows the estimated leakage outflow, which is\r\nconstructed as the difference between the virtual inflow measure-\r\nment and the total estimated demand for Area C. Three different\r\nstrategies for the demand estimation are used in Area C. First,\r\nonly the measured demand at the AMRs is subtracted [just AMR\r\nin Fig. 4(a)], which leads to an overestimation or an offset of\r\nthe leak flow because of the unmeasured customers. Second, the\r\ndemand for the whole zone is estimated based on the model as\r\ndescribed in Eq. (2) using the base demands from the BattLeDIM\r\nEPANET model (Inferred), which leads to high noise in the leak\r\noutflow estimates. Third, the AMR measurements are combined\r\nwith demand estimates for the unmeasured customers (Combined).\r\nThe last approach leads to the best leak outflow estimates with low\r\nlevels of noise as well as no offset. Clearly, four different leaks can\r\nFig. 3.
